# Arxiv Papers in cs.CV on 2026-11-23
### Ego2Wrist: Bootstrapping Wrist-View Synthesis from Egocentric Video
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29850v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29850v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 00:16:39+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 00:16:39+00:00
- **Authors**: Ziyao Zeng, Xiatao Sun, Hao Wang, Ziyu Guo, Youheng Yao, Jingcheng Ni, Shenyuan Gao, Fengyu Yang, Renrui Zhang, Bangya Liu, Daniel Rakita
- **Comment**: 27 pages (13 main + 14 appendix), 9 figures, 20 tables. Project page: https://adonis-galaxy.github.io/Ego2Wrist/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Wrist cameras provide an action-centric view that is directly relevant to robot manipulation. However, collecting human wrist-camera videos at scale is challenging due to cumbersome setups that can interfere with natural hand movements. In contrast, head-mounted human egocentric videos are much easier to scale. We introduce Ego2Wrist, which synthesizes synchronized wrist-camera views from human egocentric videos and task descriptions. Trained on approximately 940 hours of paired head- and wrist-camera recordings, Ego2Wrist jointly generates three views. To evaluate generated wrist views without ground truth, we propose hand-centric cross-view consistency (HCC), which measures consistency between generated wrist views and corresponding hand regions in the input head view. HCC ranks model checkpoints comparably to ground-truth-based LPIPS, and human evaluators prefer HCC-selected generations in 77% of responses. Evaluated on four datasets, Ego2Wrist outperforms fine-tuned WorldWander, reducing LPIPS by 28% and receiving human preference in 76% of responses. More importantly, HCC facilitates recursive self-improvement on in-the-wild egocentric videos by selecting generated wrist views for further training. Generation quality progressively improves over three rounds. These results demonstrate a scalable approach to generating real-world wrist-view human manipulation videos for robot policy training.



### LexiDet-OVD: Benchmarking Lexical Sensitivity in Open-Vocabulary Object Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29851v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29851v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 00:17:24+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 00:17:24+00:00
- **Authors**: Amit Thakur, Mukesh Singhal
- **Comment**: Accepted at WACV 2027
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Open-vocabulary object detectors localize objects from free-form text prompts, but their reliability depends on how users describe the target. Standard evaluations usually test a fixed class name, leaving prompt-induced failure modes underexplored. We introduce LexiDet-OVD, a benchmark and metric suite for studying lexical sensitivity in open-vocabulary object detection. We organize prompt sets into equivalent, conditional, and broad semantic scopes, and evaluate four detector families on fixed COCO and LVIS samples, each covering 30 classes (800 held-out COCO images and 1,000 LVIS images). Beyond mean and worst-prompt AP50, we report P10, CVaR at 20%, leave-one-prompt-out influence, detection agreement, box consistency, and confidence variance using one-to-one multi-instance matching. Equivalent-prompt best-to-worst gaps range from 5.3 to 23.4 points on COCO1000 and from 5.1 to 29.9 points on LVIS1000-30c, showing that strong average accuracy and lexical stability are distinct properties. We compare maximum-score selection, weighted-box fusion, Prompt Consensus, and an exploratory fusion rule. Aggregation recovers performance in several settings, but no method dominates all model-dataset pairs. A controlled Detic/SHiNe comparison further shows that semantic hierarchy enrichment does not by itself eliminate lexical sensitivity. These results support reporting prompt-distribution robustness alongside conventional open-vocabulary detection accuracy.



### Escriba-OCR: A 1B Vision-Language Model for Error-Preserving Transcription of Handwritten Portuguese Essays
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29874v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CL**, cs.CV, cs.CY
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29874v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 01:08:48+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 01:08:48+00:00
- **Authors**: Tardelli Ronan Coelho Stekel
- **Comment**: 21 pages, 6 figures, 13 tables. Model: https://huggingface.co/tardellirs/Escriba-OCR-1B ; code and benchmark: https://github.com/tardellirs/escriba-ocr
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Feedback tools and research on learner writing need transcriptions of handwritten essays that keep exactly what the student wrote: misspellings, line breaks, struck-through words and insertions. Vision-language models (VLMs) read handwriting well, but they also tend to rewrite it into correct language. We measure this on BRESSAY, 1,000 handwritten Brazilian Portuguese essays, with a benchmark that adds error preservation, invented errors, edit-markup precision and recall and line structure to the character error rate (CER) and uses paired bootstrap confidence intervals. We restrict the study to open-weight models that schools can run on their own hardware. Of 22 open baselines, the best, Qwen3-VL 235B-A22B, reaches 5.6% CER on the 199 test pages; none reproduces the edit markup reliably, and every VLM replaces 39-78% of the words absent from a Portuguese dictionary with dictionary words. We fine-tune LightOnOCR-3-1B on the 647 training pages in 85 minutes on one GPU. The resulting Escriba-OCR-1B reaches 2.06% CER, 3.5 points below the best baseline (95% CI [-4.2, -2.9]); under the ICDAR 2024 competition metric it reaches 2.1% CER, below the best published page-level results (2.2-2.4%). It recovers 67% of insertions and 77% of illegible deletions, and reproduces the line structure of 97% of the pages, but still normalises 60% of likely misspellings. Three seeds agree within 0.04 CER points, and quantised to 4 bits the model runs on an ordinary CPU at about one minute per page. On 40 letters of another corpus it matches its base model (without that model's appended comments) but misreads unseen words more often; weight interpolation recovers half the gap. A teacher's review finds that 45% of the judgeable apparent corrections are errors in the BRESSAY ground truth, so dictionary-based scores likely understate preservation. We release the model, the benchmark code and the review data.



### Towards Agentic Video Corpus Moment Retrieval
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29877v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29877v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 01:14:23+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 01:14:23+00:00
- **Authors**: Chenwei Xu, Shang Wu, Zhenyu Pan, Jianshu Zhang, Pranav Maneriker, Lie Lu, Fan Du, Manling Li, Han Liu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Video corpus moment retrieval (VCMR) requires finding the video and time interval that match a text query in a corpus of videos. We present RELAY, a training-free framework for VCMR that shares memory across queries. RELAY processes a batch of queries in three stages: search with a query bank, ranking with video notes, and localization with video prompt caching. In the search stage, an embedding model scores each video by its best-matching frame. The query bank penalizes videos that score highly for many other queries in the batch. In the ranking stage, a vision-language model (VLM) writes notes on each candidate video's scenes, people, objects, and actions once. Then, a large language model (LLM) reranks the candidates from these notes. In the localization stage, a VLM predicts the time interval of the moment in the top-ranked video. When multiple queries select the same video, prompt caching lets the VLM reuse its frames. On the three VERIFIED benchmarks, RELAY outperforms the trained baselines in video retrieval R@1 without training. Without any labeled moment, its predicted intervals are shorter than the annotated ones. In VCMR, it achieves similar performance to VideoSearch-R1 on one benchmark and trails it at IoU 0.5 on the other two. Human annotators also mark shorter moments than the original annotations of the benchmarks. A single margin, calibrated on a few labeled queries, widens the predicted interval to the annotation convention of the benchmarks. With it, RELAY outperforms the trained baselines in VCMR at IoU 0.5.



### Reparameterizing Your Object Detectors for Weight Space Programming
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29882v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29882v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 01:22:31+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 01:22:31+00:00
- **Authors**: Chien-Yao Wang
- **Comment**: 29 pages, 12 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Weight-Space Programming (WSP) edits network weights without backpropagation, yet directly editing converged object detectors stalls or collapses because set prediction penalizes duplicate boxes as false positives while compact parameterizations trap weights in an isotropic Euclidean minimum. We propose Inverse-Reparameterized Object-Detection WSP (IR-ODWSP), lifting detector layers via exact function-preserving operators (E, C) into decoupled moment, 2D-DCT, PCA, and Stiefel-Procrustes subspaces. Solving winner-exclusive updates and folding back (Delta Params=0%, Delta FLOPs=0%) induces an anisotropic Riemannian metric, improving all 26/26 MS COCO settings across 17 YOLO26, EdgeCrafter, and RF-DETR detectors (up to +0.31 APs), +1.42 mAP cross-basin COCO-O, zeroimage repair (+2.95 mAP), and label-free weather adaptation (+2.79 AP). Without retraining, IR-ODWSP supports -100% to +100% latency budgets across bridged contraction, 1.00x folding, dynamic (IR-ODWSP-DYN), and static (IR-ODWSP-SG) graphs: -10% retains 51.62 AP / 33.48 mAP, while positive budgets scale monotonically (+0.30 AP at +10%, +1.12 on RF-DETR-N; 54.83 AP at +100%; 55.91 AP / 35.44 mAP in IR-ODWSP+). Source code is released at https://github.com/WongKinYiu/ODWSP.



### Segment Anything In-Context via Visual Demonstrations
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29896v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29896v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 01:51:09+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 01:51:09+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuzhu Wang, Manni Duan, Hongliang Ye, Shu Kong
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We present the Segment Anything Model via Visual Demonstrations (SAYD), a foundation model for in-context segmentation. Given visual demos comprising prompt images and their segmentation masks, SAYD segments coherent regions (e.g., objects or their parts) in a query image by establishing visual correspondences between the prompts and the query. SAYD generalizes to previously-unseen segmentation tasks, including those requiring tacit visual knowledge or involving concepts that are difficult to articulate unambiguously in language. We train SAYD on 12 public datasets originally curated for panoptic segmentation using our proposed techniques, including a lightweight mask decoder for prompt-agnostic mask generation and a learning mechanism for recognizing novel query classes absent in the prompts. These innovations enhance both computational efficiency and out-of-domain generalization. Consequently, SAYD significantly outperforms existing methods of in-context segmentation and open-vocabulary segmentation across 24 out-of-domain benchmarks, spanning natural, biological, ecological, cosmological, medical, pathological, and satellite imagery.



### Motion Mamba 2.0: Autoregressive Diffusion Mamba for Long-Horizon Humanoid Control
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29897v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29897v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 01:51:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 01:51:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Yue Huang, Tianshan Zhang, Zeyu Zhang, Jayeon Kang, Akide Liu, Sungjoon Choi, Dacheng Tao, Hao Tang
- **Comment**: Code: https://github.com/AIGeeksGroup/MotionMamba2.0. Website: https://aigeeksgroup.github.io/MotionMamba2.0
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Long-horizon, real-time controllable motion generation is essential for interactive animation and humanoid robotics, where an agent must respond to changing instructions and spatial goals while moving. Existing autoregressive diffusion methods generate motion incrementally, but bounded attention caches limit access to distant history, while repeated denoising introduces substantial inference overhead. We present Motion Mamba 2.0, a Mamba-based root-body diffusion architecture that integrates local attention, resumable recurrent states, and persistent content memory (PCM) for long-horizon controllable motion generation. PCM transforms compressed motion history from Mamba recurrent states, together with state changes and text-conditioned features, into dynamically updated content slots accessible to local attention. To enable efficient inference, we further develop an execution scheme tailored to this architecture, combining CUDA Graph replay, fused kernels, and execution-flow simplification while preserving the canonical history update. Evaluations on retargeted HiPHI demonstrate accurate terminal control over rollouts of up to 39.52 s, while short-horizon comparisons on BONES Seed and retargeted HumanML3D show that the model also retains strong spatial control and text-motion matching. On an RTX 4090, the optimized execution reduces median complete-window latency from 1685.35 to 276.14 ms, achieving a median paired speedup of 6.138$\times$ while preserving identical outputs and committed states in the tested rollouts. Moreover, we conduct extensive real-world evaluation on the Unitree G1, demonstrating sequential text-guided action composition, expressive motion, and interactive locomotion through keyboard and mouse control.



### Bridging the Gap between Continual Learning and Test-Time Adaptation with Domain Preview
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29905v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29905v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 02:00:29+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 02:00:29+00:00
- **Authors**: Frédéric Li Combeau, Nicolas Michel, Toshihiko Yamasaki
- **Comment**: 37 pages, 4 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Domain shift is a major problem for training machine learning models. While Continual Learning (CL) assumes distribution shift during training and Continual Test-Time Adaptation (CTTA) considers new domains at test time, setups that require explicit online adaptation to domain shift during both phases remain under-explored. We propose continual learning with domain preview (CL-P), a new combination of CL and TTA where both train and test data suffer from distribution shift. The training phase of CL-P is analogous to domain-incremental learning while the testing phase consists of a TTA phase on a future domain (which we call preview), and an evaluation phase on all seen domains. The model must then adapt to new domains at train and test time. In this paper, we evaluate mainstream CL and TTA methods that adapt only at train or test time, as well as their naive combination. Experimentally, TTA heavily damages the domain on which the CL method has just trained and increases forgetting. Notably, this damage mostly comes from the BatchNorm statistics shared by all domains. In that sense, we propose DOSE (Dosing by Online Statistics of Each domain), which allows a seamless combination of any CL and TTA methods by adaptive domain inference at test time, where the adaptation is applied to each image in proportion to the probability that it comes from the preview domain. Through extensive experiments with ResNet and ViT backbones, various combinations of CL and TTA baseline methods and datasets considering diverse domains, we show that DOSE matches or improves on CL methods alone in all 24 settings and on the naive combination in all but one. The code can be found in appendix.



### Generalizable Variable-Rate Adaptation for Image Coding for Machines
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29914v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.IV**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29914v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 02:11:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 02:11:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Yui Tatsumi, Hiroshi Watanabe
- **Comment**: Accepted to 2026 IEEE International Conference on Visual Communications and Image Processing (IEEE VCIP 2026)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Image Coding for Machines (ICM) aims to preserve task-relevant information for machine vision under limited bitrates. However, most ICM models are trained for a fixed operating point, and extending them to variable-rate compression is challenging because their training objectives differ across tasks and model designs. In this paper, we propose a loss-agnostic distillation framework for variable-rate ICM. A pretrained fixed-rate ICM model is used as a frozen teacher, while a student model learns variable-rate behavior through importance-adaptive quantization and distillation-based optimization. This enables continuous bitrate control with a single model while preserving task-relevant latent components, without requiring the original task-specific loss or downstream task labels. Experiments based on existing ICM models demonstrate that the proposed method provides continuous bitrate control and achieves competitive recognition performance compared with the fixed-rate models.



### SpectralRoute: Adaptive Spectral Routing for Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Hyperspectral Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29919v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29919v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 02:18:40+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 02:18:40+00:00
- **Authors**: Qingmei Li, Xiaoya Fan, Juepeng Zheng, Jianxi Huang, Haohuan Fu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation enables the recognition of arbitrary categories through natural language descriptions and has recently been extended from natural images to remote sensing. However, existing approaches primarily focus on three-channel optical imagery or limited-band multispectral data, while pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) struggle to directly exploit the rich spectral information of hyperspectral imagery (HSI) without additional adaptation. More importantly, different land-cover categories rely on distinct spectral cues, which may be weakened by uniform fusion. To address these challenges, we propose SpectralRoute, a training-free framework for open-vocabulary hyperspectral segmentation. Specifically, we construct complementary spectral views from HSI and enrich category descriptions with external spectral knowledge, enabling frozen vision-language models to extract diverse spectral-semantic responses. We then introduce a class-adaptive spectral routing mechanism that selectively amplifies discriminative spectral responses for each category, overcoming the limitations of uniform fusion. Furthermore, physical spectral priors are incorporated to refine predictions for visually similar land-cover categories. Experiments on three hyperspectral benchmarks demonstrate that SpectralRoute consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods without additional training.



### Dynamic Gaussian Scene Reconstruction by Integrating Events and Structural Priors
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29920v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29920v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 02:18:47+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 02:18:47+00:00
- **Authors**: Zihao Yu, Guozhen Liu, Jie Yan, Jialiang Liu, Panlong Tan, Qinglin Sun, Hao Sun
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Existing approaches to event-based dynamic Gaussian reconstruction rely on two assumptions for support augmentation and reliability estimation. First, existing Gaussians can absorb reconstruction errors. Second, photometric optimization has converged sufficiently to estimate reliability using analytical variance. Both assumptions can fail under fast motion. When the first fails, regions without Gaussian support cannot generate densification candidates. When the second fails, Gaussians in moving regions where optimization has not converged may be excluded as motion references. We propose Event-Structure Collaborative Dynamic Gaussian Reconstruction (ESC-DGR), using event observations independent of both conditions. Events first identify changes that require additional support, placed only where rendered depth is valid. The augmented representation still needs reliable motion references. Online rendering statistics and historical event evidence therefore propagate displacement estimates from reliable Gaussians to weakly observed neighborhoods. These references describe dominant motion at the frame-interval timescale. Target-time events therefore further condition bounded position residuals for active Gaussians. Support placement, reference selection, and residual prediction thus operate on the same evolving representation within one optimization run. For event-driven monocular dynamic scene reconstruction, our method achieves the best reconstruction quality among the evaluated methods on the E-D3DGS benchmark. Relative to E-D3DGS, dynamic-region PSNR (dPSNR) improves by 1.02 and 1.45 dB on synthetic and real scenes, respectively. The total dynamic Gaussian count across eight synthetic scenes decreases by 8.5%. Although mean frame rate decreases by about 21%-25% relative to E-D3DGS, rendering remains above 80 FPS in all twelve scenes. Code: https://github.com/zh-yu1/ESC-DGR.



### Benchmarking VLM Medical Hallucination across 5 Modalities
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29934v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29934v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 02:50:36+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 02:50:36+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhixing Cao, Yusen Peng
- **Comment**: 5 pages, 6 figures, preprint
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Hallucinations in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) within the medical domain remain a difficult and not well-understood problem, and it is still unclear where these models break down. Although several benchmarks targeting medical hallucination have been introduced, they often lack broad coverage across different medical imaging modalities. We introduce Multi-Med, a medical hallucination benchmark containing 1,000 image-question pairs spanning five distinct modalities. Using this benchmark, we perform an in-depth experimental study of 10 widely used VLMs across five modalities and four question categories. Our findings indicate that model performance differs substantially depending on the imaging modality and question type, and that the differences among the best-performing models are not statistically significant.



### KGLP-Net: Kalman-Reliable Global--Local Learning With Group Fisher Pruning for Hyperspectral Image Classification
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29944v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29944v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 03:06:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 03:06:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Wenshuai Deng, Wen Xie, Abdel Hamid Soliman, Qianzheng Zhao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Accurate hyperspectral image (HSI) classification requires both the effective integration of broad spatial context and fine-grained spectral--spatial information surrounding target pixels. However, the reliability of these complementary representations may vary considerably across heterogeneous scenes, and uncertainty estimates obtained during feature fusion are rarely exploited to guide subsequent model compression. To address these challenges, we propose Kalman-Reliable Global--Local Learning with Group Fisher Pruning (KGLP-Net), a unified framework that couples uncertainty-aware feature fusion with reliability-guided structured pruning. Specifically, an asymmetric dual-view architecture employs a Global Mamba Encoder to capture long-range spatial dependencies and a Local CNN Encoder to extract fine-grained spectral--spatial features from target neighborhoods. To adaptively integrate these complementary representations, Kalman-Reliable Fusion (KRF) estimates their relative uncertainties in feature space and performs a Kalman-inspired posterior update, treating global features as a contextual prior and local features as an observation. Building upon the resulting posterior uncertainty, Kalman-Reliable Group Fisher Pruning (KRGF) quantifies group-level reliability through normalized posterior precision and integrates it with Group Fisher importance to identify and prune Kalman-coupled channel groups, enabling structurally consistent model compression. Experiments on four public HSI datasets demonstrate that the resulting Compact48 model, with a hidden width of 48, achieves the highest overall accuracy among ten representative competing classifiers. Compared with the unpruned Dense64 model, Compact48 reduces the number of parameters, FLOPs, and inference time by 38.5%, 34.8% and 37.6% on average, respectively, while incurring only a 0.03-percentage-point decrease in mean overall accuracy.



### When Should We Trust Medical Image Models? Evaluating Confidence Reliability under Cross-Dataset Shift
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29955v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29955v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 03:20:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 03:20:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Haoran Zhang, Sarah Lam, Yuting Lin, Nuo Chen, Mingyang Li
- **Comment**: 7 pages, 1 figure, 2 tables. Course research project, CS585 Image and Video Computing, Boston University, Spring 2026
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Deep learning models for chest X-ray analysis often achieve strong performance on benchmark datasets, but their reliability under cross-dataset deployment remains unclear. In clinical practice, models trained in one hospital system may be applied to data from another, where distributional and labeling differences can affect both prediction accuracy and model confidence.   We conduct a controlled empirical study of model confidence under cross-dataset transfer. We train ResNet-50 and DenseNet-121 on CheXpert and evaluate them on MIMIC-CXR across five experimental settings, focusing on Pneumothorax and Pleural Effusion. We measure predictive performance using AUC and confidence reliability using Expected Calibration Error (ECE), Brier score, and average confidence on incorrect predictions, all reported with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals. We further evaluate post-hoc temperature scaling fit on the source validation set and use Grad-CAM to examine the mechanism behind cross-domain performance differences.   Cross-domain AUC drops 7-13 percentage points, and the best in-domain model is not the best cross-domain model. Source-domain temperature scaling produces marginal ECE changes but systematically increases confidence on incorrect predictions across all four model-pathology combinations. Grad-CAM reveals that DenseNet relies on a concentrated spatial prior that fails to transfer, while ResNet's more distributed attention generalizes better. These findings argue for cross-site validation and safety-specific calibration metrics in medical AI evaluation.



### CARE-3D: Reinforcement Learning for Evidence Grounded Agentic Reasoning in Situated 3D Scenes
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29969v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29969v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 03:36:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 03:36:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Xiaoqian Hu, Ruihong Qiu, Yanran Tang, Sen Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Situated 3D reasoning requires embodied agents to interpret objects, scene geometry, and viewpoint dependent spatial relations. Existing methods learn grounded interactions from supervised traces, but largely imitate prescribed evidence acquisition patterns rather than optimizing the support that the returned evidence provides for the correct answer. Moreover, outcome rewards provide limited credit for intermediate tool choices and may leave infrequent tools underexplored. An effective agent should instead adaptively decide what to ground, which tool to invoke, and how to assess the utility of returned evidence. To this end, this paper presents CARE-3D, a reinforcement post-training framework for adaptive grounded reasoning. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on grounding instructions first establishes valid interactions, after which Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) optimizes the agent while keeping the 3D grounding module frozen. CARE-3D combines Counterfactual Attribution of Returned Evidence for evidence sensitive credit assignment, and Group Coverage Sampling to preserve tool coverage. Experiments on MSQA and Beacon3D show consistent gains over SFT and GRPO using only answer and format rewards, especially in spatial reasoning and grounding answer coherence. Our code is available at \href{https://github.com/xiaoqian-UQ/CARE-3D}{CARE-3D}.



### FASCIA: Seeing Beneath the Surface with Queryable, Personalized Anatomy in Any Pose
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29970v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29970v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 03:36:12+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 03:36:12+00:00
- **Authors**: R. James Cotton, Tim Unger, Lucinda Williamson
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We present FASCIA (*Functional Anatomical Structures and Composition via Implicit Atlas*), a Momentum Human Rig (MHR)-anchored framework that predicts and poses a person's internal anatomy from their external body shape and skeletal proportions. FASCIA maps a single 290-structure population template onto each individual through a smooth, invertible warp. As people with the same shape can differ internally, a body-dependent Gaussian prior predicts both average warp coordinates and their inter-subject variability. The warp deforms a learned base template into bones, muscles, organs, and fat depots. Extending MHR skinning into the interior enables direct supervision of this model across four automatically labeled CT cohorts and one whole-body MRI cohort. On 82 held-out CT scans, FASCIA reaches a mean volumetric Dice (volume agreement) of 0.48 on named bones and 0.63 on muscles, keeps the majority of bones and muscles in one piece, and yields calibrated voxel probabilities. On 33 held-out whole-body MRI subjects, FASCIA's pointwise field improves tissue overlap over HIT across all compartments, predicts between-person differences in whole-body adipose percentage ($R^2 = 0.88$ vs. $0.38$ in women and $0.54$ vs. $-0.01$ in men), and recovers segment centers of mass within 1.1 mm. Because it needs only an MHR fit, FASCIA can be driven from images, video, or any system that outputs MHR. This opens the door to fitting individualized calibrated musculoskeletal digital twins to video motion data.



### One Size Doesn't Fit All: Input Adaptive Class Activation Mapping for Improved Visual Explanations
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29993v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29993v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 03:55:36+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 03:55:36+00:00
- **Authors**: Ayesha Sahar, Tahir Mustafa Madni, Tehseen Zia, David Windridge, Uzair Iqbal Janjua, Waleed Umar
- **Comment**: 20 pages, 5 figures, 5 Tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Despite their widespread use in tasks such as image classification, object detection and semantic segmentation, the limited interpretability of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) can undermine trust and hinder adoption in safety-critical applications. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) methods seek to address this by providing human-understandable explanations for model predictions. Class Activation Maps (CAM) constitute one such widely used XAI technique for visualizing discriminative regions. However, existing CAM methods may exhibit limitations in channel weighting, localization, and adaptability across network architectures potentially resulting in noisy or imprecise explanations. To address these issues, we propose ADAPT-CAM, a gradient-based, parameter-adaptive CAM framework. The method combines first-order gradient energy with activation-weighted second-order curvature to derive channel contributions. It further incorporates adaptive local contrast normalization and knee-guided soft channel selection to refine the resulting explanations. Importantly, key parameters are estimated independently for each input, allowing the framework to adapt to variations in feature representations across network architectures. We validate ADAPT-CAM on ImageNet and Oxford 102 Flowers across CNN and ViT architectures. Quantitative results demonstrate competitive or superior performance against established CAM methods across multiple evaluation metrics. In parallel, qualitative comparisons illustrate its ability to highlight discriminative image regions. Further parameter analysis and ablation experiments examine the behaviour and contributions of the individual adaptive components. These findings demonstrate the applicability of ADAPT-CAM across different architectures and datasets.



### Antiphony: A Dual-Process Agent for Deepfake Video Detection with Real-Video Calibration
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29999v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.CR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29999v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:03:18+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:03:18+00:00
- **Authors**: Xinyu Yan, Yurong Hao, Zherui Li, Yebo Wu, Tiantong Wu, Jiaming Zhang, Zihao Zeng, Zheng Lin, Zijie Wang, Pengpeng Qiao, Yang Cao, Wei Yang Bryan Lim
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reliable video deepfake detection requires generalization to unseen generators while limiting false alarms on authentic footage. Although existing detectors capture diverse visual cues, they may produce overconfident errors under generator shift, limiting the reliability of confidence-based fusion. Threshold calibration is further complicated by shifts in authentic capture domains and the lack of future-generator examples. To address these challenges, we present Antiphony, a framework that combines calibrated fusion of four visual experts with selective investigation. Authentic videos serve as a shared reference for score fusion and threshold calibration, enabling threshold updates without target deepfakes. For this expert-fusion stage, we establish finite-sample false-alarm control when the scoring procedure is fixed independently of calibration and authentic samples are exchangeable. To refine decisions near the calibrated threshold, a tool-using multimodal investigator examines these videos and contributes an additional fusion score. We train the investigator on generator-held-out predictions and counterfactual expert outputs, to improve robustness to unreliable expert evidence. Across six benchmark protocols, Antiphony achieves a mean benchmark score of 88.3%, exceeding the strongest evaluated MLLM and conventional detector baselines by 14.7 and 11.5 points, respectively. It also outperforms ARGUS by 20.2 points on average and by 3.6 balanced-accuracy points on FaceVid's 20 unseen generators.



### MORSE: Optimizing Streaming Memory Design via Open-World Auto-Research
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30011v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30011v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:11:45+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:11:45+00:00
- **Authors**: Ce Zhang, Jianshu Zhang, Jinxi He, Xiyuan Yang, Pan Wang, Han Liu, Katia Sycara, Yaqi Xie
- **Comment**: Project page: https://zhangce01.github.io/morse/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: A streaming video system must decide in a single pass what evidence to retain under bounded frame storage, before it knows which details a future question will require. Recent recursive self-improvement methods automate the design of the harness that stores and retrieves this evidence. They revise the harness in response to failures, but the editor must still devise each repair from its prior knowledge and a predefined set of tools. A failure that recurs across videos, however, may point to a problem that prior work has already addressed. We introduce MORSE (Memory Optimization with Recursive Self-Evolvement), an open-world auto-research framework that uses recurring failures to guide an online search for solutions. Its editor groups failures in training traces by cause, then searches papers, model cards, and code for methods it can adapt or pretrained perception models it can use. When the editor introduces a new perceptual capability, it can also revise how the harness stores and retrieves the resulting evidence for a frozen video-language model. On held-out validation streams, MORSE first selects revisions that improve answer quality, then seeks faster revisions that preserve those gains within a specified tolerance. Across three streaming video benchmarks, MORSE consistently achieves the highest answer accuracy within each frozen-backbone comparison. On OVO-Bench, our Qwen3-VL-8B harness answers queries in 0.30 seconds on average while achieving 75.1% accuracy, 3.1 percentage points ahead of the next-best evaluated harness using the same frozen backbone.



### FlexGaze: Weakly Supervised Gaze Estimation at Scale
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30012v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30012v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:12:51+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:12:51+00:00
- **Authors**: Zongjian Li, Mohammad Soleymani
- **Comment**: 23 pages, including 12 pages of supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Most gaze estimation models are trained on data collected using specialized equipment. The limited diversity of subjects, scenes, and head poses in these datasets hinders generalization to new conditions. We present FlexGaze, a family of 3D gaze estimators pretrained with weak supervision from gaze labels (pseudo-labels) automatically generated from in-the-wild images. We use off-the-shelf models to predict 2D gaze targets and scene depth, then combine these predictions to generate 3D gaze labels for approximately 3.24 million head instances. At inference, FlexGaze predicts gaze from a head crop alone and can handle profile and rear views without requiring face detection or head pose estimation. We use data augmentation during training to improve robustness to variations in crop position and scale. Pretrained using only these generated gaze labels, FlexGaze generalizes to unseen benchmarks. After supervised fine-tuning on public gaze datasets, we evaluate FlexGaze on eight benchmarks under a unified evaluation protocol. Our models match or outperform publicly available gaze estimators with several times as many parameters on most benchmarks. We release our dataset of generated gaze labels, all trained models, and code via https://github.com/ihp-lab/FlexGaze.



### 3AGameFactory-Motion: Agentic Rigging and Reliable Complex Motion Generation for Diverse 3D Assets
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30013v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30013v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:16:51+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:16:51+00:00
- **Authors**: Bohan Zeng, Kaixin Zhu, Yifan Yang, Dongge Yu, Zihao Wu, Yifan Dai, Zhou Liu, Junbo Niu, Xiaochen Ma, Shiran Ge, Zimo Meng, Junzhu Liang, Huanyao Zhang, Jinhang Liu, Tianqi Xiao, Jinhao Zhang, Tianyi Bai, Zhixiong Zeng, Penghao Zhao, Yuanbo Peng, Key Zhang, Wentao Zhang
- **Comment**: 19pages, 10 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Animating arbitrary 3D assets from instructions requires both structural inference and motion control, yet existing motion benchmarks mostly cover conventional human or animal skeletons with simple instructions. We introduce DivDynBench, which evaluates rigging and motion generation across six morphology categories, with instructions requiring multi-phase part coordination and explicit timing, angle, repetition, and trajectory constraints. With large language models, we systematically compare three common paradigms: specialized motion generation, video generation followed by motion recovery, and direct agent-driven generation. We analyze their control capabilities, cross-morphology adaptability, and generation costs. Guided by this comparison, we propose an Agentic Motion Framework in which an agent writes compact parametric skeleton and motion programs, and a deterministic executor performs rigging, kinematic solving, and geometric correction. Compact parameter control reduces generation overhead and improves rigging reliability and motion-control accuracy, providing practical guidance for deploying complex 3D motion generation.



### ReplaySAM: Zero-Shot 3D Particle-Pack Segmentation with SAM 2
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30016v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30016v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:18:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:18:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Bogong Wang, Yulai Zhang, Philipp D. Lösel, Andrew Kingston, Mark Knackstedt
- **Comment**: 22 pages, 5 figures. Source code: https://github.com/bogongwang/ReplaySAM
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: X-ray computed tomography (XCT) provides non-destructive three-dimensional (3D) images of particle packs, making their internal structure accessible for quantitative analysis. Extracting particle-level measurements from these volumes first requires identifying and separating individual particles, a task that remains challenging in dense, irregular packs. Existing workflows often require manual correction or task-specific training, while promptable models need a strategy for automatically locating individual particles and recovering their instance masks. We present ReplaySAM, which adapts the pretrained Segment Anything Model 2 (SAM2) to three-dimensional particle-pack segmentation without additional training. It generates point prompts from foreground geometry and fuses SAM2 predictions across orthogonal slice sequences to recover particle instances. On XCT volumes of real quartz and iron-ore particle packs, ReplaySAM achieves higher mean instance Dice scores than expert-assisted watershed and nnInteractive under the evaluated protocols. ReplaySAM thus provides a training-free framework for 3D particle-pack instance segmentation, with geometry-based prompting that eliminates manual prompt placement.



### MOVER: Planning Beyond Motion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30022v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30022v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:24:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:24:06+00:00
- **Authors**: Xiangyu Li, Yu Jiang, Tianyi Wang, Liang He, Qixing Huang, Zhaomiao Guo
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Residual world models forecast a driving scene through its temporal change, so feature components that stay constant over time vanish from the residual pathway. We ask whether evidence with weak temporal change, such as a waiting pedestrian or a stopped vehicle, can be used more effectively for planning, and introduce MOVER, a complementary event-routing mechanism. A training-time teacher measures how each spatially grounded event query responds when a past frame is removed from shared history and contrasts that sensitivity with the event's pooled temporal residual, prioritizing events that depend on history but receive weak residual evidence. A student predicts the resulting priorities from unperturbed history, so the teacher adds no inference cost. Under fixed total routing mass, one distribution scales a low-rank future bird's-eye-view update and biases trajectory-conditioned event reading. On nuScenes, MOVER reduces ResWorld's UniAD-style average L2 from 0.65 to 0.55 m and its collision rate from 0.23% to 0.12% without direct planner ego status (0.50 m and 0.09% with it), adding 4.1 ms per frame. Matched controls show that the complementary teacher outperforms either signal alone and that the allocation acts mainly during training. Adapted to BridgeDrive-LEAD, MOVER reaches a 96.52 +/- 0.65 driving score over three seeds on the 220-route Bench2Drive closed-loop benchmark, exceeding matched controls in each paired seed by a margin within seed variation.



### QSRadar: Query-Conditioned Sparse Radar-Camera Fusion for Efficient 3D Object Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30023v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30023v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:25:05+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:25:05+00:00
- **Authors**: Chenhao Yan, Hongyu Liu, Xiang Cai, Yifei Sun, Zhe Zhang
- **Comment**: Radar-camera fusion, 3D object detection, autonomous driving
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reliable 3D object detection for autonomous trucks requires both the semantic detail provided by cameras and the geometric and motion cues measured by 4D radar. Existing radar-camera detectors commonly construct global radar bird's-eye-view (BEV) representations, whose computational and memory costs grow with the perception range. Sparse query-based detectors offer greater efficiency, but global feature construction can obscure point-level radar geometry and motion information. We present QSRadar, a query-conditioned sparse radar fusion framework for efficient long-range 3D detection. Built on a Sparse4Dv3-style visual-temporal detector, QSRadar retrieves a compact set of raw radar points around each evolving object hypothesis. The selected points are represented in an anchor-aligned coordinate frame, augmented with motion residuals, radar cross section, and sweep timing, and aggregated by a query-conditioned set encoder. Radar neighborhoods are retrieved again after each anchor refinement, coupling evidence selection with object estimation. Under the official MAN TruckScenes validation protocol, QSRadar achieves 38.09 mAP and 52.20 NDS, compared with 37.08 mAP and 50.78 NDS for our reproduced Sparse4D-Radar global Radar-BEV baseline. In matched tests on an RTX 4090 D, QSRadar reduces latency by 45%, peak memory usage by 49%, and GFLOPs by 43%.



### Teaching How to Reason, Beyond What to Answer: Evidence-Faithful On-Policy Distillation for streaming video understanding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30040v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30040v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:47:16+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:47:16+00:00
- **Authors**: Mengnan Liu, Sanping Zhou, Kun Xia, Jiaxuan Lin, Gang Hua, Le Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Moment Retrieval (MR) aims to localize the temporal segment in a video that corresponds to a given textual description. However, a textual query often provides only a sparse semantic description of the target event, whereas the video contains rich and fine-grained visual variations. This mismatch creates an inherent gap between textual semantics and visual content, making a single textual input insufficient for precise cross-modal alignment. As a result, when multiple temporally adjacent or semantically similar segments exist, the model may struggle to distinguish the target moment from confusing candidates. To address this challenge, we propose a Multi-Perspective Auxiliary Representation (MPAR) framework, which aims to bridge this alignment gap by enriching a single textual input with complementary visual cues from multiple perspectives. Specifically, MPAR consists of three key components: (1) Auxiliary Representation Initialization (ARI) constructs multiple learnable representation slots from the original query, preserving the query intent while preparing flexible carriers for subsequent visual cue injection; (2) Visual-Induced Semantic Structuring (VISS) injects target-specific visual attributes into each representation while preserving semantic coherence with the original query; and (3) Multi-Perspective Semantic Aligner (MPSA) aligns each auxiliary representation with corresponding video regions to enhance discriminative localization. By constructing multi-perspective auxiliary representations and guiding the model to attend to relevant visual regions across these representations, our model achieves more accurate moment localization. Extensive experiments on MR benchmarks demonstrate that our MPAR outperforms state-of-the-art methods, validating the effectiveness of MPAR.



### SWAM: State-aware World Action Model for Multi-Agent 3D Environments
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30042v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30042v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:49:25+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:49:25+00:00
- **Authors**: Jenna Kang, Bingxuan Li, Colin Groth, Anatolii Evdokimov, Sai Zhang, Qi Sun
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Interactive world models generate virtual environments autoregressively from player actions and visual history. However, visual history alone cannot reliably capture the evolving states of independently acting non-player characters (NPCs) in real games, particularly when they are outside the camera view. Recent multi-agent world models address this challenge through joint multi-view generation or persistent shared state, but often rely on external observations, like maps or scene geometry, or predefined state transitions. We present SWAM, a State-aware World Action Model that separates agent-state evolution from autoregressive visual generation in interactive 3D environments. A lightweight state model propagates character dynamics from actions without consuming generated frames, combining motion priors with learned scene-dependent corrections. The predicted states then guide an autoregressive diffusion transformer alongside visual history through a novel conditioning scheme. Rather than requiring an explicit scene geometry map or visibility tests, the conditioning indicates where characters may appear, allowing the diffusion model to learn their appearance and occlusion. This design enables NPCs to evolve independently of visual generation while maintaining consistent observations across visibility changes, interactions, and respawns. Evaluations in a custom Godot-based first-person shooter game demonstrate improvements in visual fidelity, NPC consistency, and long-horizon stability over existing approaches.



### PsyDrift: Healthy-Reference Latent Diffusion for Anomaly Scoring in Autism Spectrum Disorder
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30050v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV, eess.SP, stat.ML
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30050v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:56:03+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:56:03+00:00
- **Authors**: Yasaman Mohammadpour, Pouya Shaeri, Ariane Middel
- **Comment**: Extended version of a paper accepted at the CVPath-Vision4Neuro Workshop at WACV 2027. Includes expanded related-work and future-work discussion
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Individual heterogeneity and acquisition-site variability complicate neuroimaging-based characterization of autism spectrum disorder (ASD). We propose PsyDrift, combining ASD/healthy-control-supervised functional-connectivity representations with healthy-reference latent diffusion. An encoder produces 128-dimensional representations, and a denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) learns exclusively from healthy training latents. Distance from a diffusion-denoised reconstruction provides a continuous deviation score. We evaluate 1,102 subjects from all 20 ABIDE-I sites using five-seed leave-one-site-out cross-validation. The classifier and DDPM achieve mean-site AUROCs of $0.798 \pm 0.003$ and $0.718 \pm 0.013$, respectively; uncertainties denote seed standard deviations. Matched healthy-reference scorers on the same representation achieve 0.68-0.71; the statistical significance of diffusion's modest numerical advantage remains unestablished. Within ASD, scores show weak associations with ADOS Total (Spearman $ρ= 0.108$, $p = 0.038$) and full-scale IQ ($ρ= -0.138$, $p = 0.002$), without adjustment for age, sex, site, or head motion. Frozen evaluation without retraining or hyperparameter adaptation yields pooled AUROCs of 0.69 on ABIDE-II and 0.61 on ADHD-200, providing preliminary evidence of cross-cohort and cross-disorder transfer. These results demonstrate healthy-reference deviation scoring within an ASD-supervised representation; incremental predictive and clinical value remains unestablished.



### Bi-Refine: Semantic Verification-Guided Progressive Refinement for Moment Retrieval
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30055v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30055v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 04:59:16+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 04:59:16+00:00
- **Authors**: Mengnan Liu, Sanping Zhou, Kun Xia, Jiaxuan Lin, Yuxuan Wu, Gang Hua
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Moment Retrieval (MR) aims to identify the temporal segment in an untrimmed video that corresponds to a natural language query. Existing approaches commonly refine temporal predictions within the localization pathway, without explicitly verifying candidate-level semantic consistency. Moreover, directly reusing semantic scores as feedback can cause over-correction when the verification signal is unreliable. To address these issues, we propose Bi-Refine, a semantic verification-guided progressive refinement framework that formulates MR as a closed-loop verification-and-refinement process. A Heuristic Localization Branch first generates candidate moments, while a Semantic Verification Branch evaluates their query consistency in a semantically enriched latent space. Query supervision further trains the verifier with the same candidate moment but different queries, encouraging it to distinguish the matched query from semantically similar mismatched queries. The resulting verification features are incorporated into a Progressive Refinement Loop that updates both query representations and multimodal features. To suppress potentially harmful updates, a lightweight reliability estimator gates query updates and reweights candidate contributions in feature feedback. A convergence-based adaptive stopping strategy additionally terminates unnecessary refinement during inference. Experiments on three standard MR benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and state-of-the-art performance of our method.



### FireDesmoke: Fire Smoke Removal via Real Smoke Transfer and Semantic Rectification
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30057v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30057v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:01:35+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:01:35+00:00
- **Authors**: Chih-Chung Hsu, Rong-Lin Jian
- **Comment**: 12 pages
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Fire smoke removal must restore dense and medium smoke regions while preserving the clear (smoke-free) content. Existing methods either leave the smoke or alter the visible content, and generative models may hallucinate people or remove flames. Real fire smoke is spatially local: the degradation and its corruption of semantic features are both concentrated in the smoke regions. We propose FireDesmoke, built on this locality: Real Smoke Transfer calibrates smoke fields on real-fire recordings and transfers their spatial layout onto clean images to synthesize training pairs, and Semantic Rectification uses pixel features to rectify the tokens of a frozen foundation encoder where smoke corrupts them. With matched training budgets, replacing the transferred pairs with SmokeBench pairs lowers dense-smoke PSNR by 0.74 dB, and removing Semantic Rectification leaves clear regions 1.31 dB below the input. On what is, to the best of our knowledge, the first paired dataset of real fire smoke for image restoration, with 1,500 test frames from a held-out viewpoint, FireDesmoke improves dense- and medium-smoke PSNR by 3.73 and 2.63 dB while keeping clear regions at the input level (+0.41 dB); no other compared method gains at least 1 dB in dense smoke without lowering clear-region PSNR, and no hallucinated person is found in its outputs. It achieves state-of-the-art overall results on this benchmark and, trained on SmokeBench, the highest PSNR with the second-best SSIM and LPIPS.



### Beyond Binary Correctness: A Surrogate for Confidence Learning in Large Language and Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30059v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30059v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:04:26+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:04:26+00:00
- **Authors**: Chen Li, Xiaoling Hu, Chao Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Confidence estimation for large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) aims to assess how likely a particular generated response is to be correct. We investigate how learning this assessment can benefit from the model's predictive reliability on the underlying query. Binary correctness supervision, however, assigns identical labels to occasional errors and frequent failures, as well as isolated successes and consistent success. These outcomes can arise from substantially different predictive behavior under stochastic decoding. We propose a confidence-learning framework that supplements realized response correctness with query-level predictive reliability. We estimate reliability through repeated generations under a fixed model and decoding configuration, then combine the empirical success rate with individual correctness to construct a graded confidence surrogate. The surrogate differentiates responses sharing the same correctness label while preserving the average predictive reliability of the sampled response pool. To learn this target, we condition an independent confidence estimator on the query and its full generated response, and optimize backbone LoRA adapters together with a projection and scalar confidence head using mean squared error. Repeated sampling is required only for supervision construction; inference scores an individual response without additional generations. We establish ordering and mean preservation properties of the surrogate and derive a selective-risk bound connecting population surrogate prediction error to the error rate among accepted responses. We evaluate the framework across language and vision-language models, examining confidence calibration and selective prediction.



### Regularizing Hard Examples Improves Adversarial Robustness
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30065v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30065v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:13:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:13:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Hyungyu Lee, Saehyung Lee, Ho Bae, Sungroh Yoon
- **Comment**: 48 pages, 16 figures, 22 tables. Published in the Journal of Machine Learning Research (JMLR)
- **Journal**: Journal of Machine Learning Research 26(20):1-48, 2025
- **Summary**: Recent studies have validated that pruning hard-to-learn examples from training improves the generalization performance of neural networks (NNs). In this study, we investigate this intriguing phenomenon -- the negative effect of hard examples on generalization -- in adversarial training. Particularly, we theoretically demonstrate that the increase in the difficulty of hard examples in adversarial training is significantly greater than the increase in the difficulty of easy examples. Furthermore, we verify that hard examples are only fitted through memorization of the label in adversarial training. We conduct both theoretical and empirical analyses of this memorization phenomenon, showing that pruning hard examples in adversarial training can enhance the model's robustness. However, the challenge remains in finding the optimal threshold for removing hard examples that degrade robustness performance. Based upon these observations, we propose a new approach, difficulty proportional label smoothing (DPLS), to adaptively mitigate the negative effect of hard examples, thereby improving the adversarial robustness of NNs. Notably, our experimental result indicates that our method can successfully leverage hard examples while circumventing the negative effect.



### FolioWeaver: A LayerGraph-Grounded Agent for Layered Image Generation and Editing
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30067v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30067v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:17:48+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:17:48+00:00
- **Authors**: Wentao Lu, Shengzhe Cui, Xihao Wu, Dingxin Yan, Yixuan Qin, Pengfei Fang
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 5 figures, 6 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Layered image generation separates images into compositable layers, allowing users to manipulate individual elements independently. However, such asset-level editability does not enable the generation system to interpret successive feedback, perform targeted edits, and preserve unaffected content. We introduce FolioWeaver, a LayerGraph-grounded agent that unifies layered-image generation and iterative editing through a persistent Executable Scene State. FolioWeaver constructs a LayerGraph and generates independent visual assets conditioned on a shared scene reference. An Editing Agent performs feedback-driven modifications and self-repair through scoped actions, selective layer execution, and visual verification, enabling targeted updates while reusing unchanged assets. We also publicly release LayerFolio, a dataset of 100,377 human-reviewed layered scenes spanning six visual domains, together with two evaluation protocols: AtomicQA for measuring requirement satisfaction and CBLP for assessing layer organization. Experiments demonstrate that FolioWeaver achieves superior layer organization and composite fidelity compared with direct layered-generation methods. Analyses validate the contributions of component adaptation, execution-grounded repair, and cross-domain training. FolioWeaver advances layered-image generation toward persistent, controllable, and iterative visual creation. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/FolioWeaver/LayerFolio.



### Pixel as the Common Language: Unified Multimodal Embedding via Visual Rendering
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30069v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.IR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30069v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:23:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:23:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Xu Yuan, Pangjing Wu, Wenqi Fan, Qing Li
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal embedding models map heterogeneous inputs into a shared representation space, yet text-only, image-only, and composed inputs are still presented in distinct formats and follow different routes through the model. This fragmentation prevents heterogeneous tasks from jointly optimizing the same end-to-end encoding process, limiting supervision transfer across input configurations. To address this issue, we propose unification before encoding and instantiate this paradigm in RenderEmbed. The framework uses Visual Substrate Rendering to convert heterogeneous inputs into a common visual substrate for encoding with a VLM. From the resulting visual tokens, Progressive Hierarchical Anchor Encoding produces compact retrieval representations by interleaving learnable anchors and fusing their states across layers, retaining broad context and fine-grained cues. Interventional Rendering Learning trains these representations to remain sensitive to content changes and stable across rendering variations through complementary semantic and rendering interventions. Extensive experiments on MMEB and ViDoRe v2, spanning diverse retrieval tasks, show that RenderEmbed outperforms prior state-of-the-art models at the same scale and exhibits strong out-of-domain generalization. Compared with MetaEmbed, RenderEmbed improves MMEB Overall Precision@1 by 1.7 points, with a larger 2.1-point gain on out-of-domain tasks. Transfer and scaling analyses further show that a shared visual pathway enables more effective learning from heterogeneous supervision.



### SalivaryHSI-Seg: A New Challenging Benchmark for Ex Vivo Salivary Gland Hyperspectral Tissue Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30071v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30071v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:29:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:29:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Xiaoqi Guo, Xianran Wang, Xinquan Yang, Qinyi Lu, Fangqian Zhang, Jiangyi Chen, Ming Li, Wen Ma, Linlin Shen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Salivary gland tumors are common lesions in oral and maxillofacial practice, and accurate identification of tumor regions and surrounding salivary gland tissues is important for tissue assessment, pathological sampling, and clinical decision support. Hyperspectral imaging (HSI) records continuous spectral responses together with spatial imaging information, providing a promising way to capture fine-grained tissue-discriminative information. Nevertheless, the development of salivary gland HSI tissue segmentation remains limited due to the lack of standardized benchmarks with pixel-level annotations and unified evaluation protocols. To address this issue, we introduce SalivaryHSI-Seg, a hyperspectral tissue segmentation benchmark for ex vivo salivary gland specimens, containing 119 HSI samples from 31 patients with multi-class pixel-level annotations. We establish a patient-level five-fold cross-validation protocol and evaluate different input representations, model architectures, and challenging subsets with small tumor and complex boundary. Based on this benchmark, we further propose HyBSD, a hyperspectral boundary-guided spectral-difference network for full-HSI salivary gland tissue segmentation. HyBSD exploits boundary-guided spectral-difference cues to improve tissue delineation in ambiguous transition regions. Extensive experiments show that full-HSI input provides more discriminative information than sampled spectrums, and that HyBSD achieves 74.97% mDice and 87.76% Tumor Dice, with further advantages on the challenging subsets. Dataset and code will be released.



### SF-Flow: Spectral Flow Matching with High Structural Fidelity for Hallucination-Suppressed Scientific Image Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30073v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30073v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:30:48+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:30:48+00:00
- **Authors**: Zehua Wang, Patxi Fernandez-Zelaia, Mohamed Wahib, Xiao Wang, Isaac Lyngaas, Amir Koushyar Ziabari, Ferdinando Fioretto
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Scientific image generation focuses on reproducing experimental appearance while following a prescribed spatial layout. A key challenge is acute data sparsity, which requires operating with unpaired collections of materials (real images and prescribed layouts), where no direct supervision is feasible. Work on unpaired translation has shown that it is possible to reproduce the texture and intensity variations of real images, but it is extremely hard to retain the intended phase boundaries in the prescribed locations. This paper tackles this challenge and introduces SF-Flow, a flow-matching model trained only on real material images that imposes spectral constraints during generation, producing images that are high-fidelity and consistent with the prescribed layouts. Because layouts enter only at generation time, the approach extends to layout maps outside the distribution of any available training layouts. We evaluate the method on synchrotron tomography of concrete, lens-coupled laboratory microCT of powders, and industrial XCT of additively manufactured metal. Without instrument-specific modeling, we show that SF-Flow achieves substantially higher template agreement and joint fidelity--structure score than previous methods, including contrastive unpaired translation.



### RadarOSV: Unified 3D Scene and Motion Perception from mmWave Radars
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30078v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30078v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:37:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:37:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuan Fang, Long Zhuang, Xijia Wei, Jingqi Jiang, Fangzhan Shi, Simon Julier, Kevin Chetty
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Millimeter-wave radar offers weather-resilient sensing for 3D occupancy prediction. Existing radar occupancy methods mainly model geometry, with limited joint modeling of semantics and motion. Cascaded imaging radar provides fine spatial detail but has a limited unambiguous velocity range, whereas single-chip radar offers a wider range but coarser spatial resolution. We propose RadarOSV, to our knowledge the first radar-only framework that jointly predicts occupancy, dynamic object semantics, and ground-relative velocity from raw radar data in unified voxels, by exploiting complementary measurements from both radars. Its Polar-to-Cartesian Geometry (PCG) backbone maps polar imaging-radar features into Cartesian voxels through fixed and deformable sampling, guiding cross-radar fusion for geometry and attribute prediction. Single-chip detection outputs provide class evidence, while raw Doppler spectra supply motion features and radial-velocity candidates. The velocity readout selects from these candidates using fused features to resolve aliasing, then combines the selected radial estimate with learned motion. Ego-motion compensation uses radar-estimated velocity, so inference requires no external odometry or other sensors. On K-Radar, single-frame PCG achieves 40.7% mIoU within 12.8 m, compared with RadarOcc's reported 34.3%. On the BRIDGE-Liv dataset, compared with a cascaded-radar baseline predicting the same outputs with external ego velocity, RadarOSV improves macro-averaged accuracy across four dynamic object classes within annotated object regions from 63.1% to 75.5% and reduces object radial-velocity MAE against manual Doppler annotations from 1.03 to 0.51 m/s, while maintaining geometric occupancy mIoU.



### When "A or B" Is Less Likely than A: Coherent Probabilities for Vision Decision Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30096v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30096v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:54:05+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:54:05+00:00
- **Authors**: Seongrae Noh, SeongEun Hong, HyeongYeop Kang
- **Comment**: Project page: https://seongraenoh.github.io/core/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision decision models answer a multiple-choice question about an image with one probability per option, and software uses them directly. We show that the probabilities depend on how the options are grouped: a merged option "A or B" receives on average 11.5 points less probability than the sum of A and B. This under-additivity appears at every model size from 0.85B to 26B and remains after fine-tuning on labels. We propose CORE, which asks each training question with its options regrouped and trains the answers to all these questions toward one target distribution over the original options, summed into each question's groups. On 1,050 items from seven benchmarks, CORE reduces the total variation distance between the answer to a regrouped question and the original answer summed into the same groups from 14.8 to 5.8 points on the 0.85B model, against 9.4 for the best baseline trained on the same questions. At all five model sizes, CORE reduces this distance without lowering accuracy.



### Policy-intrinsic Demonstration Curation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30099v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30099v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:55:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:55:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Sunjae Jeong, Geunhyeok Yu, Dowoon Lee, Hyoseok Hwang
- **Comment**: 15 pages (including 1 page of references and 6 pages of appendices), 5 figures, 7 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Fine-tuning a robot policy for a new target task requires target demonstrations, but only a few are available. Demonstrations of other tasks can fill this gap, and demonstration curation selects the useful ones among these candidates. Retrieval-based curation selects the candidates that are most similar to the target demonstrations. Since it does not use the policy, any candidate that looks similar receives a high score. Performance-aware curation therefore estimates the effect of each candidate on the policy. However, this requires training on the candidates and takes a long time. We propose a third branch, policy-intrinsic curation, which uses the policy and still takes little time. It estimates this effect with forward passes of the pretrained policy and needs no training. We instantiate this branch with PICKER (Policy-Intrinsic Curation from Known Errors). It selects the candidates on which the policy makes errors that agree with those on the target demonstrations. PICKER reaches success rates comparable to those of performance-aware curation and takes about as long as retrieval-based curation. Its selection is also stable across seeds.



### Agent on Guard: Self-Improving Runtime Intervention for Robust Robotic Manipulation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30101v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30101v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:58:26+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:58:26+00:00
- **Authors**: Gengming Cui, Mingfei Han, Jiawei Zhang, Mozheng Liao, Yunkai Guo, Minghao Jin, Tingwen Du, Salman Khan, Xiaojun Chang, Zhihui Li
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Robot foundation policies provide learned execution capabilities, while programming agents can reason about failures and construct recovery behaviors. Combining them effectively requires learning when intervention helps and when the policy can reliably resume. This challenge is inherently temporal: the physical world continues to evolve during reasoning, and a useful recovery may become infeasible before a decision takes effect. We introduce Agent on Guard (AoG), a hierarchical framework that improves collaboration between a frozen base policy and a programming agent through physical experience. An evolving outer planner allocates control as the physical state evolves, aligning decisions with the physical clock with attention to observation freshness, remaining recovery windows, and the time required for reasoning, recovery, and subsequent execution. Coupled inner loops jointly refine intervention timing, recovery skills, and handoff conditions. Rather than judging recovery by local correction alone, AoG uses downstream task progress, failure recurrence, final outcomes, and resource consumption to improve both recovery behaviors and scheduling. This turns temporary agent solutions into reusable collaboration capabilities that complement the frozen policy. On RoboDojo-Sim, AoG achieves a success rate of 50.57% and a task score of 57.26, exceeding the strongest evaluated baseline by 11.22 percentage points and 10.68 points, respectively. It also reduces end-to-end episode latency by 80.4% and online token consumption by 85.1% relative to the GPT-6 Astra agent.



### LMnOP: Pruning 3D Gaussian Splatting via Leave-Many-Out Importance
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30102v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30102v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 05:59:03+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 05:59:03+00:00
- **Authors**: Sunjae Jeong, Jaeseung Kim, Hyoseok Hwang
- **Comment**: 9 pages, 5 figures, 5 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: 3D Gaussian Splatting renders photorealistic views in real time. However, a single scene often needs millions of Gaussians, so these models are costly to store and render. Pruning reduces this cost by keeping a subset of the Gaussians. A common score is the leave-one-out importance, which is the loss increase when a Gaussian alone is removed. At high pruning ratios, many Gaussians are removed together, so Gaussians that are redundant alone but essential together are lost. We present LMnOP (Leave-Many-Out Pruning), which estimates the importance of each Gaussian under the removal of the other Gaussians. This leave-many-out importance is the average loss increase when a Gaussian is removed while the others are also removed at random. We compute the exact mean and variance of each pixel's color under random removal in closed form, and approximate the importance from them without sampling removal patterns. We refine each Gaussian's probability of remaining with its importance and prune once at the end. Without fine-tuning, LMnOP achieves the best results in 16 of 18 metrics on three datasets at 90% and 99% removal. It improves SSIM and LPIPS over GaussianPOP in all 13 scenes.



### Thermal-Guided Low-Light UAV Image Enhancement: A Reliability-Aware Guidance Network and a Unified Benchmark
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30114v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30114v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 06:15:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 06:15:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhicheng Zhao, Yu Wang, Jiangong Xu, Lixiang Xu, Chenglong Li, Jin Tang
- **Comment**: 20 pages, 12 figures, 9 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Thermal-guided low-light image enhancement (LLIE) is promising for nighttime unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) perception because thermal imagery remains informative under severe visible degradation. However, the degraded visible image serves as both the alignment reference and the enhancement target, making thermal guidance uncertain in its spatial correspondence and local utility under low illumination, sensor differences, and UAV motion. Misaligned or unsuitable thermal cues may introduce artifacts, while spatially varying degradation requires region-specific restoration rather than uniform enhancement.
