- **Authors**: Bo Wang, Dekang Zhu, Jian Li, Zhipeng Meng, Bo Wang, Dewen Hu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: A UAV that flies without GNSS has to estimate its absolute position and heading without any external signal. Most existing methods for UAV-to-satellite cross-view geo-localization (U-S CVGL) treat the task as image retrieval: they compress the UAV image and each satellite tile into separate descriptors, rank the tiles by their similarity, and return the center of the best tile. The output can therefore only lie on a pre-defined grid, its accuracy is limited by the tile size, and no heading is produced. Regressing the pose in a single pass removes the grid, but it leaves no chance to correct the estimate afterwards. We observe that satellite tiles are not independent samples. They are laid on the map at a fixed spacing, scale and orientation, so neighboring tiles join into one continuous satellite block with a consistent metric, and a nadir UAV view of that block differs from it by a planar rigid transform only, i.e. a 2D translation plus an in-plane rotation. We therefore reformulate U-S CVGL as solving for this transform between two images of different sizes. Position and heading then become parts of one geometric quantity, and the accuracy is no longer limited by the tile grid. We propose RigidAnchor, which solves the transform iteratively. The network encodes the two images and builds a multi-level correlation pyramid between them. At each iteration, it indexes the pyramid with every aerial cell carried through the current estimate, pools the descriptors of all cells into one hidden state, and lets two decoupled heads predict a translation increment and a rotation increment, the latter being composed rather than added. On Bearing-UAV-90K, the cross-view mean errors are 78.6% lower in position and 69.7% lower in heading than Bearing-UAV, and HSR@15 increases by 19.06 points. Code is available at https://github.com/wwsource/RigidAnchor.



### AdvMTO: Multimodal and Temporal Adversarial Object Attacks via 2D Gaussian Splatting for BEV-Based 3D Object Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30283v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30283v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 08:40:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 08:40:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhiyang Cui, Hao Tan, Ruonan Li, Shibin Wu, Junjian Zhang, Hong'an Lin, Zhaoquan Gu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Adversarial object attacks pose a threat to BEV-based 3D object detectors in autonomous driving. Existing attacks typically target a single modality, coordinate camera and LiDAR observations at the current timestamp, or model temporal persistence mainly in camera streams. Without a unified construction of current and historical observations, attacks can leave part of the detector's accumulated evidence unsuppressed, limiting their effectiveness. In this paper, we propose AdvMTO, a 2D Gaussian Splatting (2DGS) framework for suppressing detection of persistent adversarial objects. Its oriented surfels encode appearance, geometry, and opacity in one object state, from which camera images and scanline LiDAR returns are generated. AdvMTO then constructs current and historical inputs through detector-native processing, regenerating LiDAR returns in each native source sweep for fusion detectors. We optimize bounded appearance perturbations for each detector, propagating gradients through historical BEV memory for temporal camera detectors. For fusion detectors, bounded geometry refinement along local normals further perturbs both sensing modalities. Experiments on nuScenes demonstrate effective suppression of traffic-cone detection across three BEV detectors. Temporal ablations further highlight the importance of detector-native history modeling for both attack construction and optimization.



### Face Is Not All You Need: MIME Benchmark for Incomplete Multimodal Emotion Recognition
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30288v1
- **DOI**: 10.1145/3767308.3838643
- **Categories**: **cs.MM**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30288v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 08:44:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 08:44:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuxin Jia, Xing Lan, Wensong Wang, Jian Xue, Feiliang Ren, Ke Lu
- **Comment**: Published in the Proceedings of the 34th ACM International Conference on Multimedia (ACM MM '26), November 10--14, 2026, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil
- **Journal**: Proceedings of the 34th ACM International Conference on Multimedia (MM '26), 2026
- **Summary**: Multimodal Emotion Recognition (MER) is a fundamental task in multimedia understanding, where state-of-the-art methods have achieved prominent success relying on the assumption of complete, well-aligned multimodal inputs. However, real-world unconstrained scenarios often suffer from unpredictable modality degradation and information loss, leading to catastrophic performance collapse of existing MER models that heavily depend on facial cues as the core inference anchor. However, few systematic works evaluate model robustness under diverse, fine-grained incomplete settings, as prior works are limited to coarse-grained full-modality removal and neglect interpretable analysis of emotional reasoning. To bridge this gap, we formally define the Incomplete Multimodal Emotion Recognition (IMER) task, which benchmarks model generalization and robustness under multi-grained modality and emotional information loss. We further construct MIME, a dedicated IMER benchmark with 2,000 video segments covering realistic fine-grained facial-detail degradation, severe facial degradation, and extreme full-modality loss, surpassing the limited coarse-grained settings of prior works. We also propose the Chain of Emotion (CoE) analysis paradigm with tailored metrics to dissect the fine-grained emotional cue perception and reasoning process of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Extensive experiments on seven mainstream MLLMs reveal that body language and scene context act as critical compensatory mechanisms under facial degradation, while a pronounced gap exists between models' basic visual perception ability and high-level cognitive reasoning. Our work provides a focused evaluation platform and actionable insights for advancing robust multimodal emotion recognition. Our dataset and code are public at https://yuxinokk.github.io/MIME.



### Uncovering Separable and Composable Skill Mechanisms in Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30290v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30290v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 08:45:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 08:45:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Miaoyu Li, Boyang Li
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent work has sought to localize the internal mechanisms underlying visual reasoning skills in vision-language models. These methods largely overlook separability and composability in both mechanism identification and evaluation. We propose separability and composability as two important desiderata for skill localization. Separability requires identified mechanisms to contribute primarily to their target skills while having limited effects on other skills, enabling precise diagnosis and targeted intervention. Composability requires skill mechanisms to be combinable to support complex tasks, allowing a small set of mechanisms to account for broader capabilities. In this work, we propose a multi-skill interpretability framework DISCO, built around these two properties. For identification, we propose Separable Head Selection, which selects skill-specific attention heads based on their contributions across skills. For evaluation, we are the first to design metrics for measuring separability and composability of identified heads. Extensive experiments on three VLMs and four fundamental visual reasoning skills demonstrate the effectiveness of DISCO. It achieves the highest separability, outperforming the strongest baseline by 24.80, 18.38, and 13.75 points across the three models, respectively, while also achieving the highest composability on all three models.



### PinGround: Pinning Queries to Points for Parallel Grounding in Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30304v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30304v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 08:55:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 08:55:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Shuxuan Li, Jialiang Kang, Yanghao Zhou, Ziye Zhu, Zhilin Zhao, Xiao Yang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language models (VLMs) can localize through their text interface, grounding open-vocabulary objects, complex referring expressions, text lines and layout regions with strong semantic understanding. They write the boxes one after another as a single sequence, although the instances of a query, unlike the words of a sentence, form an unordered set with no sequential dependence. The number of decoding steps therefore grows with the number of instances, which makes dense scenes the most expensive. Decoding the instances in parallel avoids this growth, but streams that cannot see each other duplicate some instances and miss others. We propose \method, which writes a single point into each query as a spatial anchor and asks for the matching instance nearest to that point within a local radius, or \none. Given the image, the anchored queries are independent, so they are decoded in parallel as leaves of a prefix tree that shares the image and the query, and the model is trained on the same tree. Coarse-to-fine anchors refine only the regions where instances are found, duplicate suppression keeps one box per instance, and set-level reinforcement learning optimizes the set F1. Only the prompt and the attention mask change, not the architecture. On three different VLMs and 12 benchmarks covering detection, OCR, layout and referring, \method is at most 5.5 F1 below sequential decoding trained on the same data, while its speedup grows with instance density and reaches 21.9$\times$ on dense scenes.



### Seeing Together, Acting Apart: Shared Environmental Understanding for Multi-Robot Navigation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30310v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30310v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 08:58:12+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 08:58:12+00:00
- **Authors**: Haihong Hao, Lei Chen, Mingfei Han, Chenglong Yan, Dong An, Yuqiang Yang, Changlin Li, Minghao Guo, Salman Khan, Xiaojun Chang
- **Comment**: NeurIPS 2026
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multi-robot embodied systems are typically designed to collaborate by coordinating actions, sharing trajectories, or learning joint policies. We ask a complementary question: can collaboration emerge before decision-making, by first building a shared understanding of the environment? We introduce Shared Environmental Understanding, a policy-agnostic representation that aggregates partial egocentric observations from multiple robots into a shared latent world model. This shifts collaboration from the policy space to the representation space, enabling decentralized decision-making with shared environmental context. To this end, we propose Shared Environment Encoding and Retrieval (SEER), a framework that organizes multi-robot observations into a shared memory, retrieves relevant context via spatio-temporal similarity, and encodes it using a predictive latent world model. The resulting representations are injected into downstream navigation agents as tokens. Experimental results demonstrate that SEER captures action-relevant environmental structure and consistently improves navigation performance across both specialized VLN agents and general vision-language model agents, while preserving single-agent capabilities.



### SafeRank-ReID: Candidate-Conditioned Re-Ranking under Identity Conflicts for Multimodal Person Re-Identification
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30314v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30314v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 08:58:27+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 08:58:27+00:00
- **Authors**: Yizhi Wang, Zixuan Li, Zhuwen Chen, Feiwei Qin
- **Comment**: Accepted at ACM Multimedia Asia 2026 (MMAsia 2026). 8 pages, 5 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal Person Re-Identification (ReID) exploits complementary identity cues from different sensing modalities to mitigate the performance degradation caused by challenging imaging conditions. Existing methods typically estimate Modality Reliability at the query level, making it difficult to identify conflicting inputs that retain normal visual quality but correspond to an incorrect identity. Such inputs may receive high confidence, promote irrelevant gallery candidates, and lead to negative fusion. To address this issue, we propose SafeRank-ReID, a candidate-conditioned safe re-ranking framework for Multimodal Person Re-Identification (ReID). To capture the varying support of the same modality for different candidates, we introduce a candidate-conditioned evidence arbitration mechanism that integrates unimodal retrieval evidence, input quality, and cross-modal consistency to estimate trust and conflict logits for each modality-candidate pair. Together, these logits contribute to the normalized modality weights, allowing support strength to vary across modalities and candidates while reducing the contribution of potentially unreliable evidence. A candidate-set refinement module subsequently applies self-attention to jointly model candidate representations and predicts residual scores from their competitive relations, thereby refining the initial fused ranking. For fusion results that remain uncertain after refinement, a query-level fallback gate estimates an interpolation coefficient from the overall conflict level and cross-modal uncertainty, adaptively combining the fused scores with those of the most reliable unimodal branch. Experiments on RGBNT201 and MSVR310 show that SafeRank-ReID maintains competitive clean-input retrieval while improving robustness to Identity Conflicts across person and vehicle benchmarks.



### Video Generalized Category Discovery
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30316v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30316v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 08:59:11+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 08:59:11+00:00
- **Authors**: Bardia Safaei, Vishal M. Patel
- **Comment**: Accepted to WACV 2027
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) aims to categorize unlabeled data from both known and unknown classes using labeled data from only the known classes. While recent GCD methods have shown promise on static images, their performance degrades in videos due to temporal dynamics, larger intra-class variation, and the lack of temporal consistency in existing approaches. We introduce VideoGCD, a framework tailored for video action recognition under the GCD setting. Our method integrates (i) a partitioned Mixture-of-Experts module that encourages specialization for known and novel categories, (ii) a novel-class balancing loss to reduce bias toward known classes, and (iii) a temporal optimal transport loss that promotes consistent routing across frames. Experiments on three video benchmarks demonstrate that VideoGCD significantly improves performance over existing GCD methods, particularly on novel categories. Our findings highlight the importance of combining temporal modeling and expert specialization for video GCD.



### Accuracy Is Not Evidence: Measuring and Training Evidence Dependence in Video Temporal Grounding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30320v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30320v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:03:10+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:03:10+00:00
- **Authors**: Zijie Jin, Shuai Zhao, Haoran Tao, Chaomin Huang, Liwen Luo
- **Comment**: Preprint, 47 pages. Code, models and data: https://jinzijie-bupt.github.io/Accuracy-Is-Not-Evidence/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In video temporal grounding, a correct interval can come from finding the event, answering blind or fitting one annotation style, and Recall at IoU scores them alike. We measure evidence dependence, how much the answer rests on the event's frames. Our fixed-answer audit edits either the frames inside the annotated interval or, as a placebo, as many frames elsewhere in the same video, and compares the two drops in the annotated answer's likelihood. Supervised fine-tuning and three public checkpoints trained with IoU-reward reinforcement learning raise the accuracy of Qwen2.5-VL-7B by 9 to 20 points, partly by answering blind and by fitting one annotation. Yet their answers rest on the event no more than the base model's, and the same holds for IoU-reward training on our data at the audit's frame rate. This comparison is a difference of the model's own likelihoods, so we can train on it. A placebo contrast and a bound on the placebo drop rule out learning to react to any edit. With them, evidence dependence rises in all five base models we train, and the weight on clean supervision controls how much. Added to IoU-reward training, the objective raises evidence dependence while keeping the accuracy gain. Masking the event's frames makes the trained models decline or miss the event more often than their base; masking as many other frames does not. The dependence moves to another event when the query names it, and holds on a second dataset and under patch scrambling, an edit never used in training.



### TAED: Trajectory-Aligned Encoder--Decoder Distillation for Detection Transformers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30326v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30326v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:04:48+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:04:48+00:00
- **Authors**: Zilong He, Zhangchi Hu, Hebei Li, Yijun Pan, Yifan Zhao, Xiangyu Yu, Yansong Peng, Xinghao Wang, Dongsheng Jiang, Xiaoyan Sun
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Distilling localization knowledge into compact detection transformers (DETRs) requires consistent teacher-student query correspondence across decoder layers. However, in DETRs with encoder-based query selection, the teacher and student select top-K proposals independently. Same-rank queries can originate from different spatial locations and correspond to different objects, making rank-based layer-wise supervision unreliable. To address this problem, we propose Trajectory-Aligned Encoder-Decoder Distillation (TAED), a framework for knowledge transfer between DETRs of different capacities. Encoder feature distillation matches normalized multi-scale features to strengthen student representations and increase the number of teacher-student query pairs from the same encoder locations. Source-indexed query alignment pairs native queries from the same encoder locations and retains pairs associated with the same object for which the teacher provides reliable and more accurate final localization than the student. Trajectory-aligned localization distillation maintains these correspondences and uses teacher proposals and corresponding decoder-layer box predictions to guide student refinement, without introducing additional queries. On COCO, TAED improves DINO-R18 and two-stage Deformable DETR-R18 by 2.5 and 1.7 AP, respectively. Further gains on D-FINE and ECDet demonstrate applicability to recent real-time DETRs at no additional inference cost.



### Draft Forcing: Target-guided Speculative Drafting and Learned Verifier for Autoregressive Video Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30328v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30328v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:05:22+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:05:22+00:00
- **Authors**: Chenkai Xu, Yuji Ren, Zihui Liu, Yijie Jin, Yuhao Shen, Xin Wang, Yajing Chen, Keyang Luo, Shengju Qian, Litong Gong, Tiezheng Ge, Zhijie Deng
- **Comment**: 18 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables, including appendix
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Autoregressive video diffusion models enable streaming synthesis chunk by chunk, yet invoking the large model for every chunk remains costly. While chunk-level speculative decoding can offload chunks to a lightweight draft model, existing approaches suffer from two limitations: (1) an off-the-shelf draft diverges from the target's trajectory, compounding errors in history; and (2) they either omit verification or rely on costly post-decode aesthetic scores rather than verifying consistency against the target. We address both with Draft Forcing, a framework coupling target-guided drafting with an on-policy trained verifier. Specifically, to align the draft for high acceptance, we opt for feature-level drafting, conditioning it on multi-layer target hidden states persisted from committed chunks, whose outputs are pushed to match target trajectories with multi-aspect losses. For verification, we propose an on-policy trained feature-space verifier predicting CDF-calibrated frame-wise relative RMSE against the target. It is trained on speculative rollouts as contexts anneal from random to inference-aligned mixtures, and gates proposals without VAE decoding. On the 14B Self-Forcing target, Draft Forcing achieves $1.63\times$ DiT and $1.57\times$ end-to-end speedups on VBench with minimal quality loss, and up to $1.93\times$ DiT ($1.82\times$ end-to-end) when combined with orthogonal KV compression. It generalizes to accelerate a streaming variant of MiniMax-H3, generating over $50\%$ draft frames with competitive quality.



### AirVT-15k: A Query-Diverse UAV Video-Text Dataset and Query-Adaptive Evidence Alignment Network for Retrieval
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30339v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30339v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:15:22+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:15:22+00:00
- **Authors**: Xi Chen, Xu Chen, Xiangyang Jia, Ting Gan, Xu Zhang, Shuquan Wei
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 6 figures, 3 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Unmanned aerial vehicle video-text retrieval (UAV-TVR) requires matching language to both scene context and subtle object changes. A harbor-layout query relies on global scene context, whereas a departing-vessel query requires local functional cues and motion states. Existing benchmarks provide diverse aerial descriptions but give limited attention to distinct evidence needs within the same video. We introduce AirVT-15k, a benchmark of 15,504 UAV videos and 155,040 captions, with ten complementary bilingual captions per video describing scenes, object states, and temporal changes. We also propose AirFocus-CST, which selects query-relevant frames and patches, encodes temporal feature differences, and combines coarse-to-fine interaction (CFI) with optimal-transport alignment (OTA). Average mean recall reaches 63.67% on AirVT-15k and 24.41% on CapERA, improving over the strongest baselines by 2.96 and 1.91 percentage points, respectively. The dataset and code are available at https://chenxi000233.github.io/AirVT-15k-site/.



### Shift by Analogy for Compositional Zero-Shot Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30342v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30342v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:16:08+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:16:08+00:00
- **Authors**: Zanwu Liu, Bo Li, Guanglin Niu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Compositional zero-shot learning recognizes attribute-object pairs absent from training. An unseen pair has a semantic description but lacks an observed visual reference. We propose Shift by Analogy (SBA) to make unseen compositions visually seen through a shift-then-match process. Its core add-subtract-add rule transfers an attribute change between two seen image-text residuals on a source object to a reference residual on the target object. This shifts the target's own text feature toward its visual center; learned refinement produces a proxy for matching query images. During training, each target is excluded from its supports, and recognition is learned using references constructed from other pairs. An analogy library and same-object attention extend this construction across candidates, while joint learning integrates proxy matching with semantic and primitive evidence. Visual-center analysis confirms substantially improved correspondence for unseen compositions. Experiments across three standard benchmarks show that SBA consistently outperforms strong CZSL baselines under both closed- and open-world evaluation.



### SceneCast: Learning Indoor Scene Synthesis from Curated Synthetic Videos with LLM Reward
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30345v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30345v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:18:04+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:18:04+00:00
- **Authors**: Ziyang Zong, Canming Xia, Haoran Xu, Guang Tan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: 3D indoor scene synthesis requires generating objects to form a complete and coherent scene. Existing approaches rely heavily on manually annotated datasets, leading to high costs and limited generalization. We present SceneCast, an annotation-free framework that trains on large-scale, unlabeled videos, addressing two key challenges: (1) Videos provide only partial views of a scene, requiring the model to extrapolate unseen 3D regions, which is inherently error-prone; (2) Raw videos available online are uncurated, suffering from uncontrolled conditions and variable data quality. First, we design a Transformer-based model that predicts objects sequentially and is fine-tuned with reinforcement learning (RL), where an LLM acts as a spatial common-sense evaluator. The LLM provides reward signals through chain-of-thought reasoning and code-executable geometric checks to refine object configurations. Second, we introduce \trim, a data curation approach that generates training videos with controllable video generation models. Crucially, it retains only high-quality prefix clips via multi-criteria quality assessment with an entropy-based weighting strategy. Evaluated on curated synthetic video sets, our annotation-free approach outperforms state-of-the-art supervised methods in scene quality.



### NNS-GS: Non-Local Neural Scattering for Relightable Gaussian Splatting
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30351v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30351v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:23:15+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:23:15+00:00
- **Authors**: Shiyu Liang, Yang Gao, Lili Wang, Aimin Hao, Beibei Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Creating relightable 3D assets of translucent objects is challenging because changing the illumination at one surface location can affect the appearance elsewhere through subsurface scattering. Existing Gaussian-based scattering models typically predict scattering from local features, making this dependence difficult to capture. We present Non-Local Neural Scattering for Gaussian Splatting (NNS-GS), which learns non-local scattering together with geometry-guided local reflection. The scattering module learns how illumination from light-visible surface regions in light space contributes to the shading point in view space, conditioned on regional diffuse responses and spatial relationships. The reflection module supplies these diffuse responses and uses additional geometric guidance to better capture specular highlights and sharp shadow boundaries. To reduce component mixing and support controllable scattering appearance, anchor-based progressive training establishes the diffuse-scattering base before introducing specular reflection and supervises the components separately. Under calibrated point lighting, NNS-GS improves mean PSNR over existing methods by 2.47 dB and 0.81 dB on the synthetic and real translucent scenes, respectively. Additional synthetic comparisons further show that NNS-GS better matches the reference when adjusting scattering attenuation.



### STAR: Structure-Aware Temporal Action Recovery for Point Supervised Temporal Action Localization
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30354v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30354v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:25:04+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:25:04+00:00
- **Authors**: Yawar Rehman, Min Young Kim
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Point supervised temporal action localization is becoming the need of time in this era of ever growing and non-stop video data. Existing method mainly rely on snippet level action response to generate pseudo labels. However, these labels often suffer from boundary ambiguity, over extended proposals, and weak temporal structure. To address these issues, we propose STAR, a structure-aware Temporal Action Recovery framework for point supervised temporal action localization. STAR works by enhancing the base S model with temporal difference interaction with attention module to capture forward and backward snippet changes, and introduces the learning of start, core, and end roles to model the internal temporal structure of actions. In addition, STAR further improves the pseudo label generation through All point proposal ranking and Rolebank based structural re-ranking, which suppress neighbor crossing and structurally inconsistent proposals. Finally, temporal action detector to STAR feedback supervision transfers high quality detector prediction back to the STAR model through soft start, core, end, action, and background targets. This helps to further refine the pseudo labels. Extensive experiments on four standard benchmarks shows the effectiveness of STAR. By using I3D features, STAR achieves 67.2% at AVG@0.1:0.7 on THUMOS'14, 58.1% on GTEA, 74.6% on BEOID, and 31.7% at AVG@0.5:0.95 on ActivityNet-1.3, outperforming previous point supervised methods across multiple benchmarks.



### Boosting zero-shot learning through neuro-symbolic integration
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30358v1
- **DOI**: 10.1016/j.patcog.2025.111869
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, stat.ML
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30358v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:29:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:29:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Francesco Manigrasso, Fabrizio Lamberti, Lia Morra
- **Comment**: 15 pages, 2 figures
- **Journal**: Pattern Recognition, Volume 170, February 2026, Article 111869
- **Summary**: Zero-shot learning (ZSL) aims to train deep neural networks to recognize objects from unseen classes, starting from a semantic description of the concepts. Neuro-symbolic (NeSy) integration refers to a class of techniques that incorporate symbolic knowledge representation and reasoning with the learning capabilities of deep neural networks. However, to date, few studies have explored how to leverage NeSy techniques to inject prior knowledge during the training process to boost ZSL capabilities. Here, we present Fuzzy Logic Prototypical Network (FLPN) that formulates the classification task as prototype matching in a visual-semantic embedding space, which is trained by optimizing a NeSy loss. Specifically, FLPN exploits the Logic Tensor Network (LTN) framework to incorporate background knowledge in the form of logical axioms by grounding a first-order logic language as differentiable operations between real tensors. This prior knowledge includes class hierarchies (classes and macroclasses) along with robust high-level inductive biases. The latter allow, for instance, to handle exceptions in class-level attributes and to enforce similarity between images of the same class, preventing premature overfitting to seen classes and improving overall performance. % CHECK: original sentence had no verb ("Both class-level and attribute-level prototypes through an attention mechanism ...") FLPN learns both class-level and attribute-level prototypes, the latter through an attention mechanism specialized for either convolutional- or transformer-based backbones. FLPN achieves state-of-the-art performance on the GZSL benchmarks AWA2 and SUN, matching or exceeding the performance of competing algorithms with minimal computational overhead. The code is available at https://github.com/FrancescoManigrass/FLPN



### REACT: Response-based Expert Assembly from Contextual Trajectories
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30366v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30366v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:36:05+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:36:05+00:00
- **Authors**: Hesen Yang, Yuxue Liu, Yanzhe Chen, Weibo Zhao, Junwei Su, Dan Hu, Shenggan Cheng, Xuanlei Zhao, Wenchao Zhou, Junhua Wang, Lansong Diao, Yang You
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Dense feed-forward networks are unaffected by how their neurons are grouped: every neuron is evaluated. Sparse execution makes this grouping consequential, because each input can access only a few experts. An arbitrary partition can therefore retain pretrained weights while separating computations that an input needs together. We argue that upcycling should organize neurons according to their responses to similar inputs. To establish this correspondence, we introduce a context-guided upcycling approach REACT, Response-based Expert Assembly from Contextual Trajectories, that uses frozen denoising trajectories to connect input contexts with pretrained neuron responses. We first identify contexts after timestep-wise centering to account for distribution shifts across noise levels, then use their associated responses to allocate intact neurons to routed experts. The same contexts initialize the corresponding router entries, aligning expert contents with their intended inputs, while global activation statistics select an always-active shared expert. Layer-wise reconstruction and end-to-end adaptation refine this initialization for sparse execution. On ImageNet-1K, our approach achieves better generation quality while activating only 37.5% of the original FFN parameters and reducing total per-token activated parameters by 27.6%.



### Mind the Gaps: State-Guided Supervision Allocation for Object Detection Dataset Distillation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30369v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30369v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:37:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:37:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiehui Xie, Zhengxun Jin, Jiahao Zhang, Yuxuan Luan, Dongshen Han, Caiyan Qin, Sung-Ho Bae, Chaoning Zhang
- **Comment**: Jiehui Xie and Zhengxun Jin contributed equally. Corresponding authors: Sung-Ho Bae and Chaoning Zhang
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent advances in dataset distillation have enabled large-scale training datasets to be compressed into compact synthetic sets. However, dataset distillation for object detection remains challenging, as it requires preserving diverse classification and localization signals under limited image budgets. Existing approaches often emphasize individual-image informativeness without explicitly accounting for supervision accumulated across the distilled dataset, potentially leading to redundant supervision and uneven class-scale coverage. To address this limitation, we propose State-Guided Supervision Allocation (SGSA), a framework that formulates object detection dataset distillation as a dataset-level progressive supervision allocation problem. SGSA establishes class-scale supervision quotas and maintains a global state that tracks detector predictions accumulated from previously selected synthetic images. The remaining gaps between the target quotas and accumulated supervision guide adaptive object sampling, detector-guided canvas refinement, and marginal-gain-based candidate selection, encouraging complementary supervision across images. On MS COCO, SGSA outperforms a matched OD3 baseline by 3.73-4.06 mAP points across three image budgets (0.25%-1.0%). On PASCAL VOC, SGSA outperforms the strongest evaluated baseline by 4.53-6.90 mAP50 points across three image budgets. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of dataset-level supervision allocation guided by accumulated detector feedback under limited image budgets.



### ColonoWorld: Camera-Controlled Full-Procedure Colonoscopy Video Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30372v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30372v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:40:32+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:40:32+00:00
- **Authors**: Jihwan Hong, Sunghwan Steve Cho, Ping Shu Ho, Joo Seong Kim, Bokyung Kim, Nam-Joon Kim, Simon See, Ji Won Kim, Jaeyoung Do
- **Comment**: 22 pages, 8 figures, 11 tables. Project page: https://aidaslab.github.io/ColonoWorld
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Navigation systems in colonoscopy are advancing from guiding the endoscopist to steering the scope autonomously. Since an incorrect motion can injure the patient, developing and evaluating these systems calls for a world model of colonoscopy. Such a model must follow a commanded scope motion, keep the colon anatomy consistent, and show lesions and procedural events. Yet camera-controlled generators trained on general scenes lose the colon's structure under motion, and endoscopic generators offer no camera control. We present ColonoWorld, a colonoscopy world model conditioned on a camera trajectory and a coarse 3D tube of the colon, trained on in vivo videos. From a whole-colon tube, it generates a full cecum-to-rectum procedure, even for unseen colon shapes. It also places polyps, instruments, and irrigation at specified locations and times. ColonoWorld outperforms state-of-the-art video generators, reducing camera trajectory error by 32% and FVD by 29%. Our code and annotations will be released at https://aidaslab.github.io/ColonoWorld.



### BASE: A Backend-Agnostic Streaming Embodiment Layer for Full-Duplex Conversational Avatars
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30388v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30388v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:48:26+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:48:26+00:00
- **Authors**: Chenye Yang, Jin Jiang, Shengcai Liao
- **Comment**: 15 pages, 7 figures, 10 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Many end-to-end spoken dialogue models can now converse in real time and full duplex, and turning them into 3D avatars requires full-body gestures, listening feedback, and turn-taking. Existing methods couple the motion generator tightly to one dialogue model by reading its internal states or training speech and motion jointly. The motion module must then be retrained whenever the dialogue model changes, cannot be attached to cloud APIs that expose only audio, and entangles role, voice, and motion style. We propose BASE, a backend-agnostic streaming embodiment layer whose only interface to the dialogue model is its emitted speech. First, a conversation state manager driven solely by audio handles speaking, listening, idling, barge-in, and proactive turns on one timeline, while role, voice, motion style, and 3D appearance are set independently. Second, to run an offline 2 s flow-matching gesture model on live audio, we reformulate inference as sliding-window continuation with overlapped look-ahead ensembling, which removes seam jitter. Third, we show that the remaining streaming gap comes from errors accumulating in the self-generated motion history rather than missing future speech; our Drift-Aware History Perturbation (DAHP) reduces this gap in about 50 min on one GPU by injecting inference-like drift into ground-truth training histories, and transfers to a second generator. On BEAT2, streaming BASE reaches an FGD of 0.342, on par with the tightly coupled MIBURI (0.333), and viewers prefer its body motion over MIBURI's in 82% of forced-choice judgments. Without retraining, the same layer drives a cascaded pipeline, three open full-duplex models, and a cloud API in real time.



### LiquidGazeNet: Towards Event-Based Near-Eye Gaze Estimation under Low-Light Conditions
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30392v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30392v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 09:54:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 09:54:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Himanshu Kumar, Sanjay Singh
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Near-eye gaze estimation under low-light nighttime conditions is challenging because the quality and effective temporal resolution of conventional intensity frames can be reduced. This paper presents LiquidGazeNet, an event-only gaze-estimation framework that combines polarity-aware spike-latency encoding with a custom LTC-based recurrent module. Positive- and negative-polarity events are represented in separate channels, while exponentially decayed time surfaces retain per-pixel temporal recency across successive event windows. A shared convolutional encoder extracts spatial features from each window, and a two-layer recurrent module composed of LTC-inspired cells integrates them causally over short temporal sequences. The network is trained using a hybrid objective that combines screen-space coordinate regression with centre-referenced directional consistency. The framework is evaluated on Gaze-FELL under cross-subject and cross-scene protocols and is further assessed through event-representation, temporal-module, window-duration, and computational ablations. LiquidGazeNet achieves mean errors of 3.75 degrees and 2.41 degrees under cross-subject and cross-scene evaluation, respectively. The results indicate that polarity-aware temporal-recency encoding, together with recurrent integration, is effective for event-only gaze estimation under the evaluated low-light nighttime conditions.



### GeLiFiSH: Recursive LiDAR Keypoint Estimation with Ground and Moving Object Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30402v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30402v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 10:03:59+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 10:03:59+00:00
- **Authors**: Nikolaos Stathoulopoulos, George Nikolakopoulos
- **Comment**: 8 pages, 3 figures, 3 tables, submitted
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: LiDAR registration and loop closure often rely on sparse keypoints, which must remain repeatable across sessions, viewpoints, and sensors. To handle differing scan patterns, detectors typically operate on accumulated submaps, which introduces latency and processes each submap independently, so repeated observations are never fused. Height-based detectors also reference structure to an estimated ground, so its inconsistency across sessions propagates into the keypoints. Moreover, dynamic objects leave ghost trails that can be mistaken for static structure. We present GeLiFiSH (Geometric LiDAR Filtering with Sequential Hypothesis testing), a learning-free filter that recursively estimates persistent keypoints at scan rate. A height-structure detector runs on a rolling voxel map, and detections initialize tracks with a position, covariance, and existence probability, accepted or rejected by a sequential probability ratio test. Ground segmentation provides a sensor-agnostic height reference, and moving object segmentation removes dynamic points before map insertion. On SemanticKITTI, HeLiMOS, and HeLiPR, spanning five LiDARs and multiple sessions, GeLiFiSH attains the highest average keypoint repeatability among the compared detectors and the second-highest cross-session registration recall from keypoints alone, behind ISS which extracts four times as many keypoints. With one parameter set and no sensor-height prior, both ground and moving object segmentation modules outperform their baselines on average.



### Spatial-Zero: Self-Evolving Spatial Reasoning in Vision-Language Models from Zero Data
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30424v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30424v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 10:18:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 10:18:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Yutong Li, Haoyu Zhao, Daoguo Dong, Ziyi Ye, Yugang Jiang
- **Comment**: 19 pages, including appendices. 4 figures and 3 tables in the main text
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Spatial reasoning requires a vision-language model~(VLM) to understand metric distances, three-dimensional relationships, and viewpoint-dependent geometry. Learning these capabilities typically requires externally curated training data. This paper introduces Spatial-Zero, a VLMs' training framework that can self-evolve through autonomous spatial exploration with zero data. Spatial-Zero addresses two key challenges in autonomous spatial exploration: reliably generating valid spatial interaction tasks and providing accurate supervision for learning from these tasks. Specifically, a proposer specifies object configurations and questions, and a Coder turns them into executable scene programs that produce multi-view images and explicit scene geometry. A Geometric Oracle checks spatial constraints, visibility, and answer uniqueness and derives ground-truth labels from executed geometry; these labels supervise the Solver and ground difficulty feedback, while validation outcomes guide subsequent task generation. Across six spatial reasoning benchmarks and three VLM backbones, Spatial-Zero yields consistent gains through iterative self-evolution. After three iterations, Qwen3.5-9B improves average accuracy by 3.23 percentage points; with Qwen3.5-4B, Spatial-Zero also outperforms existing VLM self-evolution methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/YutongLi2024/Spatial-Zero



### Decompose and Describe: Narrowing the Diagnosis-to-Report Gap in 3D CT Report Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30458v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30458v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 10:47:22+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 10:47:22+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhisong Wang, Wanxing Chang, Weiwei Cao, Fenghe Tang, Bowen Shi, Ruifeng Yuan, Yong Xia, Jianpeng Zhang
- **Comment**: 30 pages, 9 figures, 14 tables, including appendices
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Automated 3D CT report generation could support radiology workflows, yet medical vision-language models (MedVLMs) often omit diagnostic findings from generated reports. We ask whether the diagnostic ability of these models carries over to free-form reporting. Across five MedVLMs, diagnostic VQA achieves higher macro-F1 than reports generated from the same CT studies and evaluated on the same disease categories. We call this task-dependent discrepancy the diagnosis-to-report gap. Conventional Report Adaptation improves reporting for most models but does not close the gap. To investigate and narrow it, we introduce DeCAD (Decompose CT Analysis and Describe), which progressively decomposes CT report generation at diagnostic, regional textual, and regional visual levels. The model identifies positive findings before writing a report, organizes diagnosis and description into regional tasks, and restricts each task to visual tokens from the corresponding region. Diagnostic and regional textual decomposition improve report-level diagnostic performance across the five MedVLMs, whereas regional visual decomposition provides limited additional gains. In a standard VLM without medical VQA pretraining, the first two levels remain effective; regional visual decomposition also provides further gains with a region-level contrastively pretrained encoder. Full DeCAD achieves state-of-the-art performance on CT-RATE and Merlin. Analyses show greater CT-token attention during diagnosis and higher recall of reference-positive findings in both the diagnostic output and final report than with Report Adaptation. These results show that explicitly organizing diagnosis improves diagnostic reporting and helps narrow the diagnosis-to-report gap.



### Rethinking the Evaluation of Contrastive Decoding for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30460v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CL**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30460v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 10:51:35+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 10:51:35+00:00
- **Authors**: Ting Chen, Geng Li, Guohao Chen, Hanjia Lyu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Contrastive decoding (CD) mitigates object hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by contrasting token distributions under different conditions to adjust token scores. However, existing generative and discriminative benchmarks primarily measure final task scores, which can be confounded by candidate filtering, changes in generation trajectories, and response biases, making it difficult to isolate the effects of the underlying contrastive adjustments. To address these limitations while complementing existing evaluations, we introduce CONCOR (Contrastive Correction Evaluation), a controlled benchmark that directly evaluates the competition between ground-truth and different types of hallucinated candidates using the Contrastive Grounding Margin (CGM). CGM also enables analysis of directional consistency, composition, and reliability of contrastive corrections. Our evaluation reveals that existing CD methods exhibit distinct correction behaviors: some consistently favor ground-truth candidates, whereas others produce strong corrections but also introduce more errors. Crucially, these differences help explain their contrasting responses to increased contrast strength, which improves some methods while degrading others. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating the contrastive correction itself, rather than relying solely on final-output performance, and CONCOR provides a controlled and interpretable framework for understanding and improving CD methods for hallucination mitigation.



### LiveMirror: Real-Time Diffusion Virtual Try-On on a Consumer GPU and Its Self-Supervised Adaptation to Indian Ethnic Wear
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30461v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30461v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 10:52:34+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 10:52:34+00:00
- **Authors**: Jigyansha Jeevan Rout, Sukanta Kumar Sabut
- **Comment**: 12 pages, 10 figures, 6 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Diffusion virtual try-on (VTO) models produce convincing garment transfers from a single product photograph, but at hundreds of milliseconds to seconds per image every deployment today is offline or falls back to AR mirrors that need a rigged 3D asset per product. We present LiveMirror, a live-camera try-on mirror that renders a flat garment photograph onto a moving person with a diffusion model on one 16 GB consumer GPU. Its core is asynchronous keyframe try-on: generation never blocks the display, every displayed frame is an optical-flow warp of the latest generated keyframe, and keyframes are replaced only through a cross-fade. The display runs at camera rate (25 FPS at 720p) while a new try-on arrives every 370-490 ms; we measure the residual flicker, give a stage-by-stage latency budget, and report which accelerations (step count, consistency LoRA, tiny decoder, compilation) keep garment prints intact. We then show that the same GPU fixes the model's biggest cultural blind spot: try-on models trained on VITON-HD and DressCode render a knee-length kurta or sherwani as a torso-length top or a dress over bare legs. A thigh-covering mask, self-supervised pairs built automatically from 24.9k on-model e-commerce photographs, and 3.2 GPU-hours of attention-only fine-tuning of CatVTON halve the perceptual error on ethnic wear (LPIPS 0.137 to 0.077 on IndoFashion, 0.135 to 0.073 on Atlas; 200 held-out pairs per source) with no regression on Western tops. A two-pass hem-aware mask then removes the remaining failure, recoloured trousers below the hem, on all but 3-4% of pairs at no training cost; in the mirror the hem comes from the previous keyframe for free. The adapted model beats FASHN VTON 1.5 on every aggregate ethnic-wear metric at about $20\times$ lower inference cost, and its weights drop straight into the mirror.



### Struct2Det: Grounding Category Structure in Visual Evidence for Few-Shot Object Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30469v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30469v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 10:58:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 10:58:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Kaixuan Chang, Huabing Zhou, Yanduo Zhang, Tao Lu, Liwei Wang, Zhongyuan Wang, Jiayi Ma
- **Comment**: 20 pages, 14 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Few-shot detectors struggle to distinguish categories that look alike but differ in local components, because a few examples rarely reveal which differences determine category identity. Category descriptions specify components and relations but do not establish their presence in an image. We propose Struct2Det, which uses category structure to guide detection and verify candidates in three stages. Relational Structure Encoding maps category graphs to component embeddings and a global condition. Component-Grounded Detection uses component queries to retrieve multi-scale image evidence that, with the global condition, modulates category text embeddings for initial detection. Verification-Guided Refinement matches candidate regions with components expected by their top-scoring categories; when class scores are close and matches are reliable, matched pairs correct region features before the shared detector predicts again. Category structure informs both classification inputs: text embeddings integrate image-wide component evidence, while region features incorporate candidate-specific matches. We also construct InduSym, with 88,912 symbol instances across 275 categories from 674 engineering drawings and a structural graph per category. Struct2Det improves few-shot-category AP over the strongest baseline by 6.02 points on InduSym and 9.68 on Digitize-PID, with the highest base-category AP on both. InduSym ablations show few-shot gains from correct category structure, image-level retrieval, and candidate verification. Further tests show gains in few-shot natural-object detection and zero-shot Chinese character recognition.



### Inclusion of obstacles in ground point classification in ALS data for terrain models used for analysis of vehicle traversability
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30471v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30471v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 10:59:53+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 10:59:53+00:00
- **Authors**: Mikael Lundbäck, Erik Wallin, Sima Mohtasami, Martin Servin, Mattias Nyström, Henrik Persson
- **Comment**: 28 pages, 17 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Classification of ALS point clouds has been studied for decades, but it remains challenging in forest contexts due to the great variety of terrain types, sensor equipment, flight parameters, and resulting point densities. Point cloud density has increased significantly in recent years, and existing algorithms, developed for lower density point clouds, may no longer be effective. It is beyond the scope of most existing algorithms to classify obstacles, such as boulders, while also distinguishing non-obstacles, such as bushes and undergrowth. We evaluate the performance of different algorithms for ground point classification in ALS data in a context where obstacles are included in the ground class, resulting in terrain models useful for offroad machinery route planning and navigation. The evaluation is performed using metrics directly on the point clouds (Precision, Recall, $F_1$-score) and on resulting terrain models (RMSE and maps of differences). The results show that there are several algorithms that perform well, but performance is highly affected by the parameters used. The best results in terms of terrain model RMSE are obtained with the CSF-algorithm, with an average RMSE of 0.08 meters. The results indicate that existing algorithms can be adapted to include obstacles in the ground class under the tested conditions.



### Target-Part Preference Optimization for Part-Aware 3D Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30472v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30472v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 11:00:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 11:00:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Zishan Qin, Rui Li, Zeyu Zhang, Sevvandi Kandanaarachchi, Lina Yao, Dong Gong
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Part-aware 3D generation requires not only globally plausible objects but also complete, well-separated semantic parts, yet high-quality part decompositions remain too scarce to support large-scale supervised fine-tuning. We study whether preference fine-tuning based on Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) can adapt a pretrained part-aware generator from limited part-annotated data without architectural changes. We rank candidates generated with different random seeds against ground-truth (GT) geometric metrics, and select the (object, target-part) examples with the largest across-seed quality gap. Each selected example stores a seed pair whose two flow trajectories share one clean GT compositional latent and differ only in Gaussian noise. We derive a reference-relative target-block preference loss from FlowDPO that compares the two trajectories against a frozen reference model. GT flow-matching losses on the target block and complete part set provide absolute supervision, while only the diffusion transformer (DiT) is updated. Human-ranked controls test an alternative ordering source, and synthetic cropping provides controlled loser targets that are incomplete by construction. Among the three training-set scales, the medium-scale setting gives the strongest result on held-out Objaverse and outperforms both the pretrained generator and the reported direct supervised fine-tuning baseline on the multi-part aggregate. Further analysis shows sensitivity to pair ordering, target-part coverage, and the balance between pairwise and GT supervision. Cross-dataset experiments show less consistent gains under distribution shift.



### Test-Time Latent Computation to Improve Visual Perception in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30480v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30480v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 11:05:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 11:05:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Chen Yang, Zheng Fang, Wenqi Fan, Jun Xia
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent work on Large Language Models (LLMs) suggests that reasoning capabilities can be enhanced at test time not only through longer token-level reasoning traces, but also through additional computation in latent space. This raises a natural question for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs): can additional computation in latent space improve visual perception at test time? In this work, we first verify that MLLMs contain stable reusable layers that can be re-forwarded at inference time without disrupting normal generation. Based on this observation, we propose \textbf{Lo}ok \textbf{Ba}ck in the \textbf{L}atent \textbf{S}pace (\textbf{Lobals}), a training-free decoding method that identifies the contiguous layer window with the strongest visual-token attention and re-forwards through it, allowing the model to revisit visual evidence in latent space. We demonstrate that this latent-space revisit strategy can improve MLLMs' performance across diverse multimodal settings, including general vision-language tasks, visual hallucination tasks, and vision-centric tasks.



### Do learned image captioning metrics generalize? Auditing duplicated samples in human-judgment benchmarks
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30488v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30488v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 11:10:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 11:10:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Xiaochen Zhang, Jin Li, Chunlong Xia, Longjiang Guo
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Learned image captioning metrics are trained and tested on human judgments, so their reported correlation is a claim about generalization. We audit this claim for the three most recent collections of such judgments, Polaris, Nebula, and Spica, matching images at the pixel level. First, test samples reappear in training sets: the Spica training set contains about two-thirds of the Polaris test set, with identical human ratings. Second, controlled retraining shows that duplicates can inflate a metric: for Pearl, the gain is confined to the duplicated samples and grows with every epoch; for DENEB, it stays below one point. The gain depends on the learner and on how often a training set repeats a sample. Third, Kendall's $τ$ over all samples is the sum of two terms, detecting captions with the lowest human score and ranking the others; the first supplies most of the reported correlation and is nearly saturated. The recent test sets cannot order Polos, DENEB and Pearl consistently, and a ridge regression on frozen features matches or exceeds all three. Fourth, we propose a protocol that ranks a learned metric only with a model trained without test samples, reports both terms, and compares metrics by paired tests. Under it, zero-shot vision--language judges rank first on the three recent test sets, and a fine-tuned judge ranks first on the three earlier benchmarks. The same analysis applies to any evaluator learned from human judgments; filter lists and code are released at https://github.com/morninghut/ic-dataset-audit .



### EgoStudy: How Does Scaling Egocentric Data Affect VLA and WAM Pretraining?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30490v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30490v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 11:12:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 11:12:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Puzhen Yuan, Yanjiang Guo, Yichen Liu, Xiang Zhu, Qingzhou Lu, Chenxin Liu, Tun Ban, Haodong Yan, Zhide Zhong, Jianyu Chen
- **Comment**: Project Page: https://puzhenyuan.github.io/EgoStudy-website
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Egocentric human manipulation data offer a scalable resource for training dexterous robot policies. However, how effectively robots can learn and benefit from these data remains unclear due to the embodiment gap between humans and robots. We present EgoStudy, a systematic, controlled study of egocentric data for robot learning. Holding the robot dataset fixed, we vary the scale and diversity of egocentric data, data processing strategies, and co-training configurations. We investigate three questions: (1) Which data processing and pretraining choices most effectively exploit egocentric data? (2) Which components of robot action prediction benefit most from scaling these data? (3) How do scaling effects differ between vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs)? Our experiments yield three main findings. First, data diversity, alignment between trajectories, and detailed language annotations are key factors in effective transfer. Second, scaling egocentric data yields larger gains in predicting end-effector translation than in predicting fine-grained finger movements. Third, WAMs perform better when egocentric data are limited, whereas VLAs exhibit greater gains as the amount of egocentric data increases. Together, these findings provide practical guidance on processing and using egocentric data to train VLAs and WAMs, while clarifying the benefits and limitations of scaling these data.



### When Do Spatial Plans Help Text-to-Image Generation? Causal Control, System Utility, and Text Responsiveness
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30514v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
