- **Comment**: Project page: https://heco-release.github.io/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Head serves a critical role in human cognitive and perceptual functions while remaining vulnerable to external impacts, making the understanding of head contact essential for human safety. Despite its importance, dense head-object contact estimation remains largely unexplored, primarily due to the absence of head-object contact data. Recently, it has been shown that synthetic data can serve as powerful training data in limited data scenario, implying the potential of synthetic data in head-object contact estimation. There are two core challenges for dense head-object contact estimation. First, head-object interactions are highly diverse, resulting in substantial variations in contact regions and patterns across different objects and interaction types. Second, head contact regions are often heavily occluded by hair, making direct visual observation of the actual contact regions difficult. To address these issues, we introduce HEad COntact estimation (HECO) with synthetic data. To handle diverse head-object interactions, we leverage semantic context from text features that encode semantic relationships between head regions and interacting objects. To resolve hair occlusion issue of actual head contact regions, we introduce occlusion-aware contact learning that learns to infer head contact beyond visible information from images via amodal inpainting. As a result, we enable head-object contact estimation with HECO trained with our synthetic dataset, overcoming the issue of absence of contact data. The codes will be released.



### Screening for nonrigidity in structure from motion using projected reprojection residuals
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30769v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30769v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 14:41:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 14:41:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Íñigo Cirauqui-Viloria
- **Comment**: 34 pages including 19 pages of supplementary material. 5 main figures and 5 supplementary figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Sparse structure from motion (SfM) assumes a static scene, yet deformation may still yield plausible reconstructions. Reprojection-error summaries discard how errors are arranged across points and views. We propose a diagnostic that first removes residual components explainable by small point and camera adjustments and simple spatial trends that change over time. It then measures alignment with fixed spatial and temporal patterns. The diagnostic uses one estimated SfM reconstruction and provides screening evidence rather than a physical deformation estimate. Across four synthetic tubular families, the diagnostic detected all eight acquisitions with prescribed motion, compared with five or six for summaries of reprojection-error magnitude. Each family's cutoff was the highest static score among the other three families, so the static acquisition with the highest score also screened positive. With the same cutoffs, the diagnostic retained positive detections in 19 of 20 reconstruction variants spanning changes in appearance and reconstruction pipeline, with one numerical abstention. These findings show that the arrangement of reprojection errors across points and views can reveal inconsistencies that their magnitudes alone miss. The four families were known during development, and the transfer variants reused two of them. Within this setting, the diagnostic offers a practical way to flag a plausible reconstruction for closer inspection using only its estimated cameras, 3D points and image observations.



### GRAIN: Shared-Budget Complementary Re-observation for Object Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30770v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30770v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 14:41:22+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 14:41:22+00:00
- **Authors**: Chengxuan Zhou, Shaoyang Zhang, Rui Qian, Juntao Cheng, Feng Liu, Wanru Song
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Re-running a frozen detector on enlarged image crops recovers small and crowded objects without retraining, yet every crop costs an extra detector call. Existing slicing and adaptive-cropping pipelines plan crops per image in isolation, even though inspection demand varies widely across an image collection. We present GRAIN, which unifies cross-image call allocation and bounded-output admission around cross-image comparable resolution-closure gains over boundary-safe crop interiors. Coarse hypotheses carry resolution deficits, crops close a geometric share of those deficits for hypotheses enclosed in their interior positive region, and multiplicative residuals discount covered evidence within a monotone submodular surrogate. Prefix sums and a global max-heap execute this collection-wide plan on CPU before crop inference, and Positive-Region Fusion aligns post-inference spatial admission with the planned interior support before class-balanced top-   selection. Across 17 frozen detectors from five families on COCO, CrowdHuman, and VisDrone-DET, GRAIN beats call-matched tiling in 100 of 102 AP@100 settings and transfers to AI-TOD-v2. With RF-DETR-S on CrowdHuman, it nearly matches two-crop tiling using one eighth of the crop calls. At fixed budgets, the surrogate orders candidate plans with Spearman correlations of 0.95 to 0.99 while the sweep attains at least 98% of the exact surrogate optimum. Code will be publicly released.



### Reconstructing 3D+t Cardiovascular Flow From Sparse Planar Velocity Fields With Neural Fields
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30775v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30775v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 14:43:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 14:43:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Viraj Patel, Deblina Bhattacharjee, Katharine Fraser
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 6 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reconstructing time-resolved three-dimensional (3D+t) cardiovascular flow from sparse planar measurements is challenging because each plane observes only the two in-plane velocity components, on a small part of the volume. We propose a plane-aware neural-field framework that learns a continuous 3D velocity field from space and time coordinates through a per-plane projection, and then investigate the potential benefits of physics constraints. We evaluate the framework on computational aortic and aneurysm datasets, and on planes resampled from a real cardiac 4D Flow MRI scan. A plain multi-layer perceptron (MLP) was more accurate than classical radial basis function (RBF) interpolation on both computational datasets. For the aorta, a physics-constrained SIREN (PC-SIREN) reduced the rRMSE further to $0.3756$, compared with $0.4273$ for the plain MLP and $0.7256$ for RBF, and it was the most accurate model in every anatomical segment of the MRI data. For the aneurysms the plain MLP remained the most accurate model, with an rRMSE of $0.07$ to $0.16$ against $0.55$ to $0.71$ for PC-SIREN. Further experiments investigated scanning configuration, number of scans, and spatial resolution. These findings show that continuous neural fields can reconstruct volumetric cardiovascular flow from sparse planar measurements, and that physics constraints have to be matched to the flow.



### AvatarAnyone: Agentic Reconstruction of Mesh-Based Layered Avatars from YouTube Video
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30777v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30777v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 14:44:23+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 14:44:23+00:00
- **Authors**: Aymen Mir, Peter Wonka, Stefanos Zafeiriou, Rıza Alp Güler
- **Comment**: Project page: https://miraymen.github.io/avataranyone
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We introduce AvatarAnyone, a method for reconstructing avatars from casual YouTube videos as layered meshes, with each garment and footwear as a separate mesh layered on top of the body mesh. In such videos, people differ in garment classes, clothing types, and layers, and each combination needs a different reconstruction. A single fixed layered reconstruction pipeline therefore fails. AvatarAnyone solves this problem with AI agents that plan a custom optimization for each person's reconstruction. The agents predict the garment classes, the clothing type, and the layering. They use these predictions to choose an initialization and stage the optimization. Because the avatars are layered meshes, they support applications such as virtual try-on and cloth simulation. We change the clothing layers of an avatar and render it in different garments and poses. We can also simulate a clothing layer as cloth over the animated body with a standard cloth simulator. Most existing avatars are one fused surface or a set of Gaussians and cannot do this. We load the avatars directly into Blender, Unreal Engine, and robotics simulators such as MuJoCo. As the avatars are meshes, they support contact modeling with other objects. We show results from around 150 YouTube videos. On four datasets, AvatarAnyone outperforms existing mesh-based methods for animatable monocular reconstruction. Our project page is available at https://miraymen.github.io/avataranyone.



### BridgeMVS: Bridging Multi-View Stereo and Monodepth via Bidirectional Dynamic Fusion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30787v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30787v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 14:49:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 14:49:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Jianfei Jiang, Haochen Yu, Qiankun Liu, Rui Yang, Hongyuan Liu, Xianglong Meng, Jiansheng Chen, Huimin Ma
- **Comment**: Accepted by NeurIPS 2026
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multi-View Stereo (MVS) and Monocular Depth Estimation (MDE) provide complementary cues for dense 3D reconstruction. While MVS leverages explicit geometry for metric depth, it struggles in challenging regions where feature matching is unreliable. Conversely, MDE provides robust structural priors but typically yields scale-ambiguous results. Existing hybrid methods mainly exploit monocular cues in a unidirectional manner, leaving the mutual enhancement between MDE and MVS insufficiently explored. To address this limitation, we propose BridgeMVS, a unified cascaded multi-stage MVS framework that bridges monocular depth features and multi-view cost volume representations through Bidirectional Dynamic Fusion (BDF). At each stage, BDF performs dynamic mono-to-cost and cost-to-mono updates, where monocular structural features guide cost volume refinement for more robust MVS depth estimation, while multi-view geometric evidence recalibrates monocular representations to improve their metric-scale consistency. Furthermore, the fused monocular and multi-view representations are progressively propagated from coarse to fine stages and constrained by stage-wise relative-scale MDE supervision and metric-scale MVS supervision, enabling cross-branch interactions to benefit subsequent high-resolution depth estimation. In addition, BDF is designed as a plug-and-play module that can be conveniently integrated into existing cascaded MVS frameworks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BridgeMVS achieves state-of-the-art performance on the DTU, Tanks-and-Temples, and ETH3D benchmarks, while simultaneously boosting the accuracy of monocular depth estimation. Notably, BridgeMVS ranks first on both the Tanks-and-Temples Intermediate and Advanced benchmarks. The source code is available at \href{https://github.com/JianfeiJ/BridgeMVS}{https://github.com/JianfeiJ/BridgeMVS}.



### PestWeave: Linking Semantic Modeling and Evidence-Guided Text Modulation through Visual Prototypes for Fine-Grained Pest Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30796v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30796v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 14:58:03+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 14:58:03+00:00
- **Authors**: Xueheng Li, Yu Wang, Yuxiang Shen, Wen Xue, Rui Li, Jie Zhang, Chengjun Xie
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 5 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Agricultural pests threaten crop yield and quality, making accurate pest recognition essential for targeted management and sustainable agriculture. Their diverse appearances complicate recognition and motivate multimodal approaches incorporating complementary species knowledge from text. However, complex backgrounds can obscure discriminative visual cues, while textual descriptions may contain information unsupported by the current image, limiting effective cross-modal fusion. To address these challenges, we propose PestWeave, a multimodal framework connecting content-adaptive visual organization with text fusion through visual prototypes. Its visual pathway augments a Receptance Weighted Key Value (RWKV) architecture with semantic token grouping and three types of auxiliary tokens, enabling content-adaptive scanning and contextual modeling while preserving spatial structure. Feature-similar tokens are reordered into adjacent scanning positions, while cluster prototype, global, and register tokens provide group-level summaries, global context, and additional interaction capacity, respectively. These prototypes also guide text modulation by assessing visual support for semantic components and regulating their relative contributions and overall fusion strength, reducing interference from unsupported descriptions. Extensive experiments on multiple pest recognition datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of PestWeave and its potential for practical pest management.



### Go Far, Look Less: Active Multi-Room Reconstruction with Persistent Goals and Selective Views
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30802v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30802v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:02:53+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:02:53+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiawei Jiang, Haonan Zhou, Wendi Hu, Jiabei Hu, Yina Xiang, Kaiyue Zhou, Gaoang Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Active 3D reconstruction aims to allocate a sparse view budget to recover complete geometry and high-fidelity renderings. However, existing methods degrade sharply in multi-room homes: myopic next-best-view scoring gives transit no credit for distant rooms and over-polishes irreducible nearby residuals, whereas dense mappers reach far rooms only by streaming thousands of frames. We propose ROAM-GS, a training-free active multi-room reconstruction framework that couples persistent goals with selective views: each decision selects a goal and a variable-horizon prefix toward it, capturing only at its endpoint. A Surface Reconstruction Memory scores poses by projecting post-update defects and viewing directions. Persistent goals credit transit with their remaining value and are released once arrivals stop measurably improving them. Without per-scene tuning, ROAM-GS achieves state-of-the-art multi-room reconstruction while preserving single-room Replica fidelity, raising mean coverage from 74% to 93% on HSSD and from 47% to 96% (+11.7 dB PSNR) on HM3D, and matching 90-minute dense mapping using $173\times$ fewer views.



### HIVE-Bench: Evaluating Patch-Level Visual Representations for Egocentric Robot Manipulation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30813v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30813v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:06:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:06:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Qiwei Liang, Zhengtu Liang, Minghao Lai, Yikeng Chen, Yiming Wang, Boyang Cai, Yuetong Fang, Taowen Wang, Baiqiao Yin, Tianxing Chen, Yue Chen, Jiaming Liang, Guangyu Chen, Shaolong Zhu, Daolin Ma, Renjing Xu
- **Comment**: 39 pages, 12 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Pretrained vision is a common start for robot learning, but most comparisons still score one pooled vector from a third-person image. Bimanual policies instead read dense patch tokens from cameras on the robot. We introduce HIVE-Bench: one shared flow-matching policy reads 20 encoders from seven families on 24 egocentric bimanual tasks, across adaptation, task sharing, and sweeps of data, model size, and layer. Keeping the patches matters. Averaging each view to one token removes most of the success, adaptation helps every pair we test, and one shared policy keeps most of the success of separate policies at a fraction of the updates. The same tokens are then scored with statistics, state and object readouts, published vision scores, and an inverse-dynamics readout. That readout of the commanded motion is one of the strongest measurements available before a policy is trained. Recognition, depth, and correspondence do not transfer reliably. Fine-tuning can improve the action readout even when other measurements move the other way, and no statistic is stable enough to replace a rollout before any deployment.



### Unpacking the Evaluators: How Configuration Shapes the Evaluation of Alignment in Explainable AI
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30816v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30816v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:08:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:08:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Gizem Karagoz, Tanir Ozcelebi, Nirvana Meratnia
- **Comment**: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026, Evaluations & Datasets Track
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Explainable AI (XAI) evaluation fundamentally requires human-centered evaluation, assessing whether explanations align with human reasoning and expert knowledge. However, such evaluations often require expert-driven manual validation and are difficult to scale, leading to the widespread use of alignment-based metrics, where attribution maps are compared against predefined ground-truth (GT) representations. Despite their popularity, alignment evaluations are implicitly bias towards multiple design choices made during the evaluation process, for example GT representations, metric families, metric formulations (soft vs. hard), and thresholding strategies. In this paper, we formalize alignment evaluation as a structured evaluation configuration space through which the influence of these design choices can be systematically analyzed. We further introduce a structured alignment evaluation framework that models interactions between XAI methods, GT representations, metric families, metric formulations, and thresholding strategies across 2D and 3D synthetic and real-world datasets. In addition, we propose a novel taxonomy of XAI output behavior based on local entropy and boundary mass ratio, enabling structured characterization of attribution patterns for alignment evaluation. Our experiments reveal systematic flows (triples of XAI-GT-metric configurations), showing that alignment outcomes provide distinct behavior patterns under certain configurations, such as soft/hard metric implementations. We further observe varying levels of threshold sensitivity across metric families and attribution structures. These findings demonstrate that alignment evaluation cannot be treated as a fixed or invariant measure of explanation quality, and provide practical guidance for designing more reliable and behavior-aware XAI evaluation protocols.



### Can Multimodal Fusion MakeSense for Embodied Vision in Degraded Sensing?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30836v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30836v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:20:31+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:20:31+00:00
- **Authors**: Cheng-Ru Chou, Shih-Yu Lai, Pei-Lin Chiang, Shou-Po Tsai, Jing-Zhe Huang, Fukuharu Tanaka, Hirozumi Yamaguchi, Bing-Yu Chen, Yu-Lun Liu, Hsun-Ping Hsieh
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Embodied vision systems rely on complementary sensors to perceive and act as conditions change. Even models that accept arbitrary sensor subsets can exhibit negative fusion: adding a sensor increases prediction error. We propose MakeSense, a multimodal fusion framework that preserves sensor-specific evidence in private streams and combines information through shared bottleneck tokens. Training pairs full and subset views of the same degraded observations, penalizing higher error with more sensors. Where geometry permits, the distance readout uses range measurements alone, while the rest of the prediction draws on all available inputs. Paired tests across five datasets reveal helpful and harmful sensor additions. MakeSense reduces negative fusion on MM-Fi. With lightweight adapters and per-dataset training, the same design improves UAV localization on MMAUD, cooperative 3D detection on V2X-Real, few-shot pose transfer to RELI11D, and command prediction on Humanoid-Everyday, where a world model trained on the frozen core's outputs forecasts future fused states more accurately than a linear baseline at longer tested horizons. MakeSense supports embodied sensing across human activity, aerial systems, robotics, and autonomous driving.



### Certified Defense against Adversarial Patches on Traffic Signs via Mask-Aware Training
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30840v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30840v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:24:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:24:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Rudra Saxena, Vishal Dev Verma, Rohan Sharma, Palani Thanaraj K
- **Comment**: 4 pages, 4 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Adversarial patches are a practical threat to traffic-sign recognition: a small sticker on a sign can change a classifier's decision. PatchCleanser is a certified defense that applies two rounds of masking and, for every image it certifies, provably returns the correct label for any patch that fits inside one mask. We show that on traffic signs the defense certifies almost nothing when paired with a normally trained classifier (1.7% to 4.8% certified accuracy on GTSRB), because a single mask can hide the small central symbol that distinguishes sign classes. We propose mask-aware training, which trains the classifier on images occluded by random pairs drawn from the defense's own mask set, with a sampling bias toward masks over the sign's centre. The defense, mask set and certification procedure are unchanged, so the guarantee is preserved. For an ImageNet-pretrained ResNet-50, mask-aware training raises certified accuracy from 4.8% to 60.0%. Under real per-image adversarial patches, the defended ResNet-50 reaches 85.7% accuracy, versus 74.6% for the original PatchCleanser with a normally trained ResNet-50.



### DyLoMo-DiT: Dynamic Looped Mixture-of-Experts Diffusion Transformers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30844v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30844v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:27:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:27:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Lernie Li, Yuanliu Liu, Pengfei Xiong, Wei Zhang
- **Comment**: 46 pages, 29 figures, 33 tables. Code, weights and project page: https://oneboxcream.github.io/DyLoMo-DiT/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) scale model capacity by increasing both parameter count and computation. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) variants expand parameter capacity while keeping per-token computation roughly fixed, but still require substantial parameter storage. In this paper, we propose Dynamic Looped MoE DiT (DyLoMo-DiT), which reuses parameters rather than adding them: a weight-shared loop repeatedly applies the middle layers, while sparse experts allocate parameter capacity across tokens. This design deepens the network without adding parameters, freeing the parameter budget for other uses, such as more experts or more distinct layers. It also allocates compute dynamically, varying the number of passes across noise levels. Experiments show that DyLoMo-DiT makes more effective use of parameter and compute budgets, with further gains from assigning more passes to low-noise steps. On ImageNet $256\times256$, DyLoMo-DiT reduces FID by 11-24% relative to DiT and 36-56% relative to DiT-MoE at matched parameter and compute budgets. For text-to-image generation, a scaled DyLoMo-DiT achieves a higher GenEval score than FLUX.1 (0.714 vs. 0.673), with less than one tenth of its active parameters (1.05B vs. 12B).



### Age and Gender Estimation from Face and Ocular Smartphone Images via Identity Transfer and Ordinal Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30850v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30850v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:29:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:29:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Fernando Alonso-Fernandez, Kevin Hernandez-Diaz, Jose Maria Buades, Josef Bigun
- **Comment**: Accepted for publication at 16th International Conference on Pattern Recognition Applications and Methods, ICPRAM 2027
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We investigate the transferability of identity-recognition representations to gender and age estimation from face and ocular images. Three CNNs of varying complexity, pre-trained for face or ocular identity recognition, are evaluated using fixed features with SVMs and end-to-end adaptation. Experiments use 11,299 frontal smartphone images from Adience, following its predefined five-fold protocol and subject-disjoint validation. Results show that domain-matched identity pre-training outperforms direct ImageNet and scratch initialisation in end-to-end training, while combining both ocular regions narrows the gap with full-face estimation, especially for age. We additionally introduce an ordinal loss that exploits the ordering of age groups and consistently improves 1-off accuracy across architectures and input regions. Across the evaluated configurations, best gender accuracies reach 88.1% for the face and 82.9% for combined ocular regions, while age estimation reaches 59.0% exact and 93.1% 1-off accuracy for ocular images, versus 57.6% and 95.2% for the full face. To our knowledge, these are the highest ocular age accuracies reported on Adience.



### CableMoE: Harm-Regularized Mean-Residual Expert Fusion for Transmission-Corridor Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30859v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30859v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:35:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:35:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Shima Salehi
- **Comment**: Submitted to IEEE TPEC 2027
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Semantic mapping of transmission corridors benefits from heterogeneous LiDAR and image-guided predictors, but replacing a strong ensemble with a learned router can damage rare electrical assets. We introduce CableMoE, a post-hoc mixture of four frozen segmentation experts. Its deployable prediction is a calibrated residual around availability-aware probability averaging, while a harm penalty discourages extra loss on points that the mean already classifies correctly. A matched component study shows why each design choice is needed: an unconstrained learned mixture loses 1.032 mIoU points relative to probability mean, harm regularization restores 0.359 points at fixed capacity, and removing an unnecessary point-wise gate slightly improves accuracy and stability. With three zones for fitting, two disjoint zones for residual-strength selection, and router-untouched t5b5 for final reporting, CableMoE obtains 84.906% mIoU and 98.140 % overall accuracy. This is +10.000 points over SPT and +0.263 over probability mean; all three seeds improve the latter. Across pylon, conductor, structural-cable, and insulator classes, macro-IoU rises from 84.517% for SPT and 97.153% for probability mean to 98.144 %. The results support conservative residual composition, not a claim of a new segmentation backbone or universal state of the art



### XFreq: Geometry-Aligned Cross-View Residual Learning for Sparse 3D Ultrasound Reconstruction
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30869v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30869v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:41:28+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:41:28+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuhuan Lu, P. Ellen Grant, Davood Karimi
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Three-dimensional (3D) ultrasound provides valuable volumetric context for anatomical assessment, motivating reconstruction from widely available two-dimensional (2D) slices. Sparse slices leave much of the volume unobserved, even when their acquisition poses are known. Existing methods interpolate observed intensities or incorporate learned 2D image features into 3D reconstruction, but their complementary roles in predicting smooth structure and residual details remain less explored. We introduce XFreq, a geometry-aligned framework for sparse 3D ultrasound reconstruction. It uses known slice poses and finite footprints to query 2D image features at 3D locations, complementing splatted intensities and sampling geometry. A low-frequency decoder predicts a smooth 3D field from the splat representation, while a high-frequency decoder additionally incorporates the queried 2D features to predict residual details. A learned gating module regulates the residual contribution based on geometry and cross-slice feature variation. We evaluate XFreq on an in-house fetal brain dataset and the public CETUS cardiac dataset, using slices resampled from 3D ultrasound volumes and fivefold cross-validation. Among the compared methods, XFreq achieves the highest PSNR and SSIM in most settings across both datasets, reaching 26.82 dB PSNR on CETUS with 32 input slices. Ablation studies further show the contributions of local feature querying, geometric cues, and residual prediction, with gains generally increasing as more input slices are used. Our findings highlight the value of geometry-aligned cross-view feature integration for reconstructing 3D ultrasound volumes from sparse 2D slices.



### Learning the Next Best Move with Visual Diffusion Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30879v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.GR**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30879v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:46:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:46:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Amir Razlighi, Ali Mahdavi Amiri, Yiorgos Chrysanthou, Andrey Voynov, Daniel Cohen-Or
- **Comment**: project page: https://amirhossein-razlighi.github.io/next_best_move
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In this paper, we show that visual diffusion models can learn to predict the next best move (NBM) in tasks requiring sequential reasoning and lookahead, without explicitly exploring future steps or exhaustive searching at inference. Previous work demonstrated that these models can generate complete solutions to geometric and reasoning problems. Here, we show that simple fine-tuning on visual state--NBM pairs enables a pretrained model to select actions whose value depends on consequences several steps ahead. Each move is predicted directly in image space from the current state, without an explicit symbolic representation or search procedure. Repeated NBM predictions then construct a solution one decision at a time.   We demonstrate this capability on chess puzzles and tile-based pathfinding, and show that it extends to Connect Four, Go and billiards. In chess, the model predicts legal moves and finds the best move far more often than by recalling similar training positions, including piece sacrifices whose benefit emerges only through later tactical continuations. In pathfinding, it constructs shortest paths on uniform-cost maps and cost-optimal routes across terrain tiles with different traversal costs, choosing longer routes when they are cheaper overall. These costs are learned implicitly from examples, without numerical cost supervision. These results show that direct visual move prediction can support globally informed decisions and successful sequential problem solving.



### A High-Resolution UAV Benchmark Dataset and Baseline Evaluation for Teak Crown Segmentation in Western Ghats Tropical Forests
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30888v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30888v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:54:10+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:54:10+00:00
- **Authors**: Anand S Sahadevan, Sunita Arya, Anup Kumar Das, Bimal Kumar Bhattacharya, S Manthira Moorthi
- **Comment**: 5 pages
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Accurate teak-tree segmentation from high-resolution drone imagery is critical for sustainable plantation management, yet lacks dedicated benchmarks. We introduce TeakTreeNet, a comprehensive dataset and evaluation protocol for binary teak-tree segmentation at native 2cm resolution. Four state-of-the-art architectures: U-Net, PSPNet, DeepLabV3, and SegFormer were rigorously benchmarked across scratch and ImageNet-pretrained settings with three-seed reproducibility. SegFormer establishes new SOTA performance with F1 0.938 +/- 0.002 (Teak), IoU 0.968 +/- 0.001 (Teak), and Accuracy 0.986 +/- 0.001, outperforming all pretrained CNNs. Domain-adaptive training suffices, as the scratch SegFormer outperforms ImageNet pretrained baselines, avoiding large-scale pretraining overhead. Comprehensive analysis reveals Transformer's superior boundary delineation for dense plantation scenarios. TeakTreeNet provides researchers with 6391 expertly annotated drone patches (224x224) and establishes reproducible evaluation protocols, enabling direct comparison of future methods. Our benchmark demonstrates SegFormer's optimal balance of accuracy, efficiency, and deployability for operational forestry monitoring of teak plantations for India's western tropical forests.



### Speak When You See It: Proactive Task Assistants Should Wait for Evidence, Not the Clock
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30895v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30895v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 15:57:25+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 15:57:25+00:00
- **Authors**: Bohe Chen
- **Comment**: 31 pages, 10 figures, 27 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Proactive assistants that guide people through physical tasks must decide, every few seconds, whether a step is complete and whether a mistake has occurred. We find that two benchmarks for this task largely reward knowing when rather than seeing what: a timer that never looks at the video, raising an alarm at 25 s and confirming each step at 30 s, reaches a mistake F1 of 0.373 on Ego-MC-Bench, above every published result (at most 0.27). We formalize this as the video-blind frontier, the best trade-off between completion accuracy and mistake F1 that policies reach from the elapsed time, the instruction and training statistics alone: every published per-step result on Ego-MC-Bench lies inside it, and so do all twelve zero-shot models we run on QICD. To move beyond it, we introduce Evidence-Contrastive Training (ECT): fine-tune on the benchmark's own questions, and pair its positives with negatives from the same recording at the same elapsed time, so that elapsed time cannot separate the two sides of a pair. Trained on 209 QICD videos in about 15 GPU-hours, a 2B model with ECT crosses the frontier on both benchmarks in all four seeds; transferred to Ego-MC-Bench without adaptation, it confirms 59% of mistake-free steps in time, against 30% for a published model with the same backbone trained on about 25k synthetic counterfactuals and real recordings. Its decisions follow the video beyond what timing explains: at equal elapsed time it tells a finished step from an unfinished one in 90-92% of test pairs, where the instruction's typical duration reaches 65%, rescaling the time stamps between the frames leaves it essentially unchanged, and swapping in another recording, of the same recipe where one exists, cuts its completion accuracy by two thirds. The code and model weights will be publicly released.



### World Models as Native Latent Action Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30898v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30898v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:01:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:01:06+00:00
- **Authors**: Qinyue Li, Weijie Qiao, Qingyi Tao, Zerui Gong, Zhe Yang, Quanzhou Li, Taolin Zhang, Jinglin He, Zhonghua Wu, Chen Change Loy
- **Comment**: 19 pages, 7 figures, 6 tables. Project page: https://pawteam-eai.github.io/native-lam/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Large and diverse video sources, such as egocentric human video and heterogeneous robot recordings, carry rich dynamics but come either without actions or without a shared action space. Latent action models (LAMs) can recover these dynamics, but they train their inverse- and forward-dynamics models (IDM, FDM) from scratch, trading off pixel reconstruction against latent capacity and accuracy. Pretrained video world models natively generate high-fidelity dynamics, yet represent them densely, entangled across all layers. Viewing the world model as a LAM, we show that these dynamics can be extracted with minimal changes to the model. Native LAM operates one pretrained video diffusion transformer as both IDM and FDM, with learnable dynamics registers as the only causal bridge between the two, and trains on counterfactual temporal pairs that differ in motion alone, so that the registers distill a video's dynamics into a compact native latent action. We further propose a pretraining paradigm for vision-language-action (VLA) policies, Pretraining Actions from World models (PAWlicy), in which Native LAM serves as a dynamics teacher on large-scale video; unlike world action models, it never generates video. Native latent actions outperform existing LAMs on the LARY benchmark in both action classification and control regression, and replay the same motion on new robots, in new scenes, and from human hands to robots, although no transfer objective is used in training. With a 3.8B model, PAWlicy reaches a 93.6% average success rate on RoboTwin 2.0 trained with domain-randomized demonstrations, the highest among VLAs. On RoboDojo, it is competitive on every dimension we evaluate, and its Precision, evaluated by us, is on par with the leading leaderboard entries. On real robots, it improves over the pi_0.5 baseline on both tasks. Both stages use only public data and a publicly released world model.



### GlanceNav: Efficient Vision-and-Language Navigation with Streaming Memory and Counterfactual Rewind
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30900v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30900v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:02:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:02:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Anh Dao, Quan-Dung Pham, Danh Vinh Le, Tuyen Pham Le, Quan Nguyen, Yuzhang Shang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong policies for vision-and-language navigation (VLN). However, most of them encode a sample of past frames again at every decision and are trained on isolated decisions, so computation is redundant and the usable history is short. They also learn from their own mistakes through outcome rewards that give one scalar to a whole episode. We ask whether a navigator can encode each frame only once, at a constant cost per step, and still remember what matters and learn efficiently from its mistakes. We present GlanceNav, a streaming navigator built on a hybrid MLLM. Its Gated DeltaNet layers carry a recurrent state through the whole episode, while its attention layers keep a short window of recent observations and a small bank of keyframes, selected by how much each frame changes the recurrent state. This constant-size memory keeps the cost per step fixed and allows training on whole episodes with the same memory as at inference. Because the entire memory can also be copied in constant time, we further propose Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), a reinforcement learning stage that rewinds a drifting rollout to its closest approach to the goal, lets an expert correct a few actions, and credits decisions by the resulting change in outcome. Trained only on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE training splits, with 1,000 instructions for reinforcement learning, a 4B GlanceNav reaches success rates of 71.9% and 73.6% on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen. On R2R-CE, it outperforms the strongest previous single-RGB method trained without external data by 9.1 points, while running at 104 ms per step.



### DexLatentWorld: 3D Cross-Embodiment Latent Action World Models for Zero-Shot Robotic Manipulation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30907v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30907v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:05:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:05:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Vinay Udit Mohan, Xuchuan Huang, Rolandos Alexandros Potamias
- **Comment**: 15 pages, including supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: World models facilitate planning and control in robotic manipulation by enabling agents to anticipate the consequences of their actions. However, they typically rely on action-labelled interaction data that is costly to acquire and difficult to scale. These models are also often tied to the specific embodiment they were trained on, limiting transferability to previously unseen robot morphologies. We introduce a 3D latent-action world model that learns continuous action representations directly from point-cloud observations of retargeted human manipulation demonstrations. A contrastive objective is used to encourage consistent representations of motion across embodiments. The resulting unified latent action space serves directly as the search space for model-predictive control, where sampled latent actions condition particle-based future predictions. Executable robot commands are then recovered from the predicted geometric motion without requiring an embodiment-specific action decoder. Through extensive evaluations, we demonstrate that the learned latent actions exhibit controllable and compositional motion, transfer to unseen robot embodiments, and support zero-shot manipulation with unseen gripper geometries. Following task-agnostic adaptation, our model achieves 90%, 92%, and 60% success on reaching, grasping, and placing, respectively, compared with DexWM's reported 72%, 58%, and 28%. Finally, we demonstrate zero-shot real-world manipulation with an unseen dexterous hand, achieving an 86% success rate across 50 trials on five diverse tasks involving pushing, grasping, and lifting everyday objects. These results highlight the potential of geometry-grounded latent actions as a unified, transferable interface for world-model-based robotic manipulation. Project materials, including demonstration videos and source code, are available at https://vinaymohan0409.github.io/DexLatentWorld/.



### Replay Before You Reply: Second-Look Prompting for Unified Multimodal Encoding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30909v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30909v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:06:28+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:06:28+00:00
- **Authors**: Yicheng Zhang, Xun Zhang, Jingyuan Ma, Tianyu Yu, Yuan Yao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Solving multimodal tasks requires Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to form a unified understanding of the image and the question text. However, under causal attention in current VLMs, a token can attend only to what precedes it. Whichever modality comes first is therefore encoded under partial observation of the input, uninformed by the rest of the multimodal context. We introduce Second-Look Prompting (SLP), a training-free method that repeats the image-question pair in the input. In the repeated copy, every visual and textual token can attend to the complete input, yielding a unified multimodal encoding of the pair. Moreover, the added visual tokens contribute computation beyond re-exposing the image: even a blank image in their place can improve reasoning. Across 12 benchmarks, SLP improves the overall average score of every evaluated model, spanning nine open-source models (3B-72B) and three proprietary frontier models, with gains reaching 5.6 points for Gemini 3 Flash on MATH-Vision. Our analysis reveals two complementary mechanisms: the question guides where the second image attends, and the added visual tokens act as a prefill scratchpad whose hidden states retain answer-relevant information from the image and question. These findings highlight input presentation as a simple yet effective way to improve VLM performance.



### Do Waste Classifiers Recognise Items They Have Never Seen? Object-Disjoint Evaluation on Household and Medical Waste
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30911v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30911v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:06:40+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:06:40+00:00
- **Authors**: Nusrat Jahan, Mst. Eshrat Jahan Esha, Masuma Sadia, Saad Ahmed, Md Zahim Hassan, S M Jahangir Alam
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Automatic waste classification supports source separation, which enables recycling and keeps hazardous medical items out of household waste. Waste classification studies often report near-perfect accuracy on random image splits, which put photographs of one item in both training and test sets. To measure how well classifiers recognise items they have never seen, we built BanglaWaste-23, with 4,160 images of 172 objects in 23 household and medical waste classes from Bangladesh. Every image also records which physical item it shows. We evaluated 11 ImageNet-pretrained models and two models trained from scratch (a compact custom network and MobileNetV3-L) using object-disjoint five-fold cross-validation and a random split. On unseen objects, the best pretrained model reached a macro-averaged F1 score of 98.8%, and six models were statistically tied. The random split overstated from-scratch accuracy by 15 to 19 percentage points. The from-scratch models also missed 11 to 13% of medical items, against under 1% for pretrained models. On a second dataset from Dhaka, pretrained accuracy fell by 15 to 22 points. Classifiers should therefore be evaluated on unseen objects, with random-split results read as an upper bound. The data are on Zenodo and the code at https://github.com/saadbaust/BanglaWaste-23.



### CoSel: Cooperation as Selection for End-to-End Driving
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30918v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30918v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:10:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:10:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Yiping Li, Haohua Que, Zhipeng Bao, Wenjie Zhao, Qianwen Li
- **Comment**: 22 pages, 11 figures, 9 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Cooperative driving can extend a vehicle's awareness beyond its own sensors, but existing cooperative end-to-end methods inject shared information into the planner and rely on cooperative driving demonstrations to learn how to use it. The moments in which shared information should change a driving decision are rare in such data, providing limited supervision for learning this behavior. We argue that cooperation need not be learned by the planner. Instead, reacting to shared information requires predicting how surrounding agents will move and selecting a response from the candidate trajectories of an ego planner. We introduce CoSel, a cooperation-as-selection framework that leaves the planner and its ego-only input unchanged. A separately trained cooperative predictor uses observations from the ego vehicle, roadside units, and connected automated vehicles to predict surrounding agents and their future motion. CoSel evaluates the planner's candidate trajectories against these predictions, preserves the planner's choice when it remains safe, and selects the closest safe alternative when shared information reveals additional risk. Each safety intervention is traceable to the shared evidence that triggers it. In closed-loop evaluation, CoSel achieves a driving score of 70.7 on V2Xverse, outperforming the best compared cooperative method by 7.5 points, and the highest average driving score of 85.8 on MDrive. Under missing ego detections, the collision count of CoSel increases only from 17 to 19 in 72 scenarios, compared with 19 to 32 for an existing cooperative end-to-end model.



### CHICO: Bridging Sparse Metadata and Multimodal Evidence in Cultural Heritage Collections
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30923v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.DL**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30923v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:16:11+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:16:11+00:00
- **Authors**: Vagner Inácio de Oliveira, Paula Dornhofer Paro Costa
- **Comment**: 6 pages, 3 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Cultural heritage aggregators provide access to millions of digitized items. Yet their catalog records often contain sparse, heterogeneous metadata, with associated media links that may not lead to accessible images. Structuring and validating these resources for multimodal AI research can support reproducible experimentation. To this end, we present CHICO (Cultural Heritage Images and Catalog Objects), a multimodal dataset derived from Europeana that brings together curated catalog fields, images, and contextual information to support evidence-grounded description generation while retaining the original Europeana records. It comprises more than 60,000 cultural heritage records with associated images. Although originally developed for that purpose, it can also support multimodal information retrieval, metadata enrichment, and evidence-based evaluation.



### FGSCalib: Targetless Camera-LiDAR Extrinsic Calibration from Foreground Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30935v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30935v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:20:51+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:20:51+00:00
- **Authors**: Marcell Kégl, Csaba Benedek
- **Comment**: 12 pages, 5 figures, and 4 pages of supplementary material. Code: https://github.com/zsungi/fgscalib
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Camera-LiDAR pairs installed as fixed infrastructure need periodic recalibration, but a calibration target takes the installation out of service. We present FGSCalib, a targetless method that calibrates a stationary camera-LiDAR pair from the objects moving in its view. We only assume that arbitrary moving objects are present in the scene: each sensor separates the moving foreground from the static background, without relying on object classes. With the correct extrinsic, the LiDAR foreground points project onto the camera foreground. FGSCalib turns the camera mask into a boundary-proximity field and finds the extrinsic by two-stage simulated annealing over selected frame pairs. It needs no measured initial extrinsic (random starts give similar results) and works with both rotating and non-repetitive LiDARs. We evaluate it on 21 simulated scenarios with exact ground truth and on 72 real ones (nine camera-LiDAR pairs in eight scenes), with a reference averaged from 43 manual calibrations. In simulation the median error is 5.3 cm and 0.19°. On real data we use a tolerance of 18 cm and 1.8°, met by 93% of single manual calibrations. Keeping the lowest-cost of five runs, FGSCalib meets it in 51 of the 72 scenarios: in 20 of 24 with the Livox, where the strongest of five baselines reaches 11, and in 31 of 48 with the two rotating LiDARs, where only one baseline estimate does. Its median error is 9.5 cm and 0.9°, against 7.3 cm and 0.7° for a single manual calibration. The code and the dataset are available online.



### SIAMBA: A Pseudo-Siamese Mamba-based Deep Learning Network for Multimodal Flood Change Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30938v1
- **DOI**: 10.1117/12.3115920
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30938v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:22:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:22:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Muhammad Fasih Ishfaq, Giulio Weikmann, Lorenzo Bruzzone
- **Comment**: 9 pages, 3 figures, presented at SPIE Environmental Remote Sensing, 2026
- **Journal**: Artificial Intelligence and Image and Signal Processing for Remote Sensing XXXII; 142820K (2026)
- **Summary**: Flood change detection from bi-temporal multispectral and synthetic aperture radar (SAR) images is essential for rapid disaster response. In this context, Sentinel-2 multispectral imagery provides discriminative spectral observations, whereas Sentinel-1 SAR provides weather-independent structural information. Using these modalities jointly requires modelling temporal evidence while accounting for cross-modal heterogeneity. In particular, change detection is complicated by cloud contamination, illumination variability, acquisition geometry and speckle, and heterogeneous land-cover responses. To address these challenges, we propose SIAMBA, a pseudo-siamese Mamba-based network for bi-temporal flood change detection from paired Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 observations. SIAMBA uses dedicated modality-specific encoders with dual-pooling squeeze-and-excitation recalibration and selective state-space processing for long-range spatial context. A gated cross-modal change module derives absolute, signed, and relative log-ratio differences at multiple semantic levels, and attention-refined skip connections support progressive multi-scale decoding. Experiments are carried out on OMBRIA benchmark, which includes an independent test set and geographically diverse subsets in France, Albania, Guyana, and Timor. SIAMBA achieves 81.98% IoU, and 93.57% OA on the independent test set, reaching the highest values in the considered comparison. It also provides the highest unweighted average IoU and OA across four regional subsets spatially disjuncted. These results indicate that combining selective state-space contextual modelling with gated multiscale discrepancy learning effectively improves flood change mapping across the evaluated geographic settings.



### Diffutoon-2: Toon Shading with Video Diffusion Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30945v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30945v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:24:45+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:24:45+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuze Sun, Zhongjie Duan, Yingda Chen, Jingjing Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Toon shading renders objects with a flat, stylized appearance. Diffusion models open a new route to this task by transforming photorealistic videos into an anime style. However, existing approaches often rely on curated source and style training pairs or additional visual references. Diffutoon avoids these requirements through structure guidance and colorization, but builds on an image diffusion backbone with short-window motion modules and repeated overlapping-window denoising. We present Diffutoon-2, a video diffusion framework that learns toon shading through animation reconstruction. Two complementary modules independently reconstruct animation clips from outlines and blurred colors, with the video backbone frozen. At inference, the modules jointly guide generation using the same types of conditions extracted from photorealistic footage, requiring neither cross-domain training pairs nor external style references. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Diffutoon-2 outperforms both general-purpose and domain-specific baselines, across quantitative metrics and human evaluation.



### Self-Evolving Evaluation Environments for Computer-Use Agents
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30962v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30962v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:32:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:32:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Wenhao Wang, Panrong Tong, Quyu Kong, Pengxiang Li, Haowen Yang, chenglin Cai, Siheng Chen, Yue Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Computer-use agents (CUAs) are rapidly improving, yet their evaluation remains largely static. Existing benchmarks provide a strong foundation, but fixed difficulty ceilings, limited feature coverage, and coarse aggregate scores progressively weaken their discriminative and diagnostic value. We introduce CUA-SEE, a closed-loop evaluation environment that co-evolves tasks and executable verifiers from rollout evidence. It disentangles three objectives: benchmark evolution expands evaluation scope through cohort-calibrated difficulty and coverage growth; diagnostic evolution increases resolution through agent-conditional capability-boundary probes; and verifier evolution improves precision by translating changing requirements into state-grounded checkpoints with adversarial validation. Across multiple agents, applications, and rounds, CUA-SEE generates challenging, measurable tasks while preserving process-level evidence. Our results position adaptive evaluation as a scalable counterpart to self-evolving agents, continuously tracking what agents can do, where their boundaries lie, and how reliably progress is measured.



### Foresight Forcing: Learning Spatial Foresight for Robotic Manipulation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30963v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30963v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:32:22+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:32:22+00:00
- **Authors**: Mishuo Han, Zhixuan Liang, Zhaoyuan Wan, Wendi Chen, Jiarui Chen, Xinkai Wang, Yue Ma, Yu Zhou, Junting Chen, Yao Mu, Jun Lv, Chuan Wen, Cewu Lu
- **Comment**: Project website: https://anson2333.github.io/foresight-forcing
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Robotic manipulation policies can improve action prediction by anticipating how objects move and how their spatial relationships change. World Action Models (WAMs) learn to predict future visual observations, but their training objectives typically leave the underlying 3D geometric transitions implicit. To alleviate this problem, we introduce Foresight Forcing ($\mathcal{F}^{2}$), a WAM that learns a spatial transition representation from changes in a pretrained 3D feature space. During training, a frozen 3D foundation model extracts patch-wise feature differences from aligned current and future observations that provide dense supervision for geometry-aware feature transitions. We distill these targets into spatial transition queries, which are progressively refined from the current observation, language instruction, and proprioceptive state, and use them alongside current visual features to generate actions. At deployment, the policy predicts the queries without future observations or the 3D teacher. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin2.0 demonstrate strong manipulation performance and improved robustness, including 95.01% average success on RoboTwin2.0 Full. On six real-world manipulation tasks, $\mathcal{F}^{2}$ achieves a mean task score of 85.8%.



### Graph2Scene: Relation-Aware Text-to-Image Generation with Graph-Guided Diffusion Model
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30964v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30964v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:32:53+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:32:53+00:00
- **Authors**: Peizheng Li, Xin Ai, Guanghui Cheng, Zikang Dong, Qiange Wang, Yanfeng Zhang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Text-to-image diffusion models still struggle with complex prompts containing many elements and interactions. Existing attention-control methods mainly focus on controlling where elements appear, but often ignore how objects interact with each other. As a result, relation tokens describing interactions between elements may assign high attention to irrelevant regions, leading to missing or incorrect interactions. In this paper, we propose Graph2Scene, a training-free framework that treats scene graphs as a core control signal for diffusion generation. It represents elements as nodes and their relations as edges, and translates these element-relation dependencies into selective attention guidance. It grounds each element to its corresponding region and guides relational interactions among the involved elements. To achieve appropriate relation control without over-constraining the generation process, Graph2Scene further adapts the control duration to scene complexity and progressively relaxes graph guidance during denoising. Experiments on T2I-CompBench and ConceptMix show clear improvements in compositional generation, especially for object relations. On ConceptMix, Graph2Scene improves relationship accuracy from 0.59 to 0.84 over the strongest FLUX.1-based regional-control baseline. Results on LongBench-T2I further show its advantage on long and complex prompts.



### Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30986v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30986v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:42:40+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:42:40+00:00
- **Authors**: Yihong Luo
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Forward diffusion reinforcement learning (RL)---enabling deterministic ODE sampling without trajectory backpropagation---has grown increasingly popular for efficiently aligning diffusion models. However, we reveal that existing methods essentially rely on signed reward-weighted regression (RWR), regressing the \emph{trainable policy} velocity onto rollouts from a \emph{behavior distribution}. Unbiased baseline subtraction implicitly assumes that the policy velocity equals the behavior marginal velocity---an idealized assumption violated under classifier-free guidance (CFG) rollouts, offline/off-policy updates, and imperfect online self-consistency. This \emph{behavior--policy mismatch} breaks baseline invariance and injects \emph{baseline-induced gradient drift}, destabilizing training. To resolve this issue, we propose \textbf{I}nstantaneous \textbf{T}rajectory \textbf{D}istribution \textbf{M}atching (\textbf{iTDM}), taking the infinitesimal limit of Trajectory Distribution Matching toward a time-local reward-guided target via the posterior-score identity to yield a sample-level gradient anchored at the behavior velocity without learning an additional reward-tilted target score. Tracking this velocity with an online auxiliary denoiser guarantees exact baseline invariance at the auxiliary optimum, effectively suppressing baseline drift across both online and offline RL. Extensive experiments across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment show that iTDM achieves state-of-the-art in-domain and out-of-domain performance. Notably, on the challenging GenEval2 benchmark, iTDM converges nearly $3\times$ faster than competing methods while scaling smoothly to $0.87$ without collapse. Code is available at https://github.com/Luo-Yihong/iTDM



### Demographic Encoding is Not Exposure: Fairness and Privacy in Chest X-ray Foundation Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31014v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.IV**, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31014v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 16:59:11+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 16:59:11+00:00
- **Authors**: Sahar Askari, Amin Golnari
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Medical foundation models encode patient demographics and can reveal whether a patient was in the training set, and these properties are often treated as one problem. We tested whether they are aligned by measuring demographic encoding, diagnostic fairness gaps and membership-inference vulnerability on the same models. We trained 200 linear or multilayer-perceptron (MLP) heads on frozen BiomedCLIP, DINOv2 and RAD-DINO embeddings, with three seeds, and audited them on CheXpert and NIH ChestX-ray14. Demographic encoding explained almost no variance in patient vulnerability, and all 72 CheXpert associations were statistically equivalent to zero. Erasing linearly decodable demographics left leakage unchanged but reduced age-related underdiagnosis gaps. Leakage depended instead on head capacity and training length. On RAD-DINO, a linear head reached a membership-inference area under the curve (AUC) of 0.694, compared with 0.907 for a 30-epoch MLP, at similar test performance. Fairness and privacy should therefore be audited separately, and linear heads or shorter training reduce leakage with little or no loss of utility. The code is available on GitHub for public use.



### Parsing Visual Latents for Multimodal Spatial Reasoning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31021v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.CL
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31021v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 17:02:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 17:02:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhuoying Yang, Yichen Guo, Kathy Jia, Zichang Su, Ayesha Kashif, Nange Hai, Jeffrey Ming Han Li, Zhewen He, Silvio Kempf, Ni Jiachen, Junjie Yang, Muhammad Hamza, Zakaria El Ouahidi, Gabriel Azpitarte, Manling Li, Yi Fang, Shuhong Liu, Mengyu Wang
- **Comment**: 18 pages, 8 figures, 12 tables. Code: https://github.com/Harvard-AI-and-Robotics-Lab/LatentParse
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Spatial reasoning across multiple views requires inferring the 3D relations between objects and the camera from 2D observations, which depends on cross-view correspondence and scene geometry that vision-language models (VLMs) do not reliably extract. Pretrained vision encoders already capture this information in their latent features, with CLIP linking objects to image regions, DINOv3 matching semantics across views, and VGGT encoding multi-view geometry. However, VLMs do not benefit from these features when we present them directly in context as grayscale feature images. We therefore propose LatentParse, a training-free framework that parses latent features into explicit spatial evidence for VLMs. In LatentParse, an analyst VLM probes the features of all three encoders with predefined tools that locate objects, track them across views, and measure motion between views. It then distills the results into a concise text report, which is provided together with the images to the VLM to answer the question. We evaluate LatentParse on MMSI-Bench, MV-RoboBench and SPAR-Bench, where it improves all five VLMs over the photo-only baseline by 0.7-2.4 percentage points on average. On MMSI-Bench, it raises Qwen3.8-27B from 34.4% to 39.5%, with the largest improvements of 19.2 percentage points on the camera-related questions. These results suggest that latent features become useful for spatial reasoning once they are parsed into relation evidence, without training. Code is available at https://github.com/Harvard-AI-and-Robotics-Lab/LatentParse.



### OmniV-Bench: Can MLLMs Comprehend Immersive Spatio-temporal Scenes?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31022v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31022v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 17:03:16+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 17:03:16+00:00
- **Authors**: Liu Yang, Huiyu Duan, Yixuan Gao, Sirui Liu, Lu Liu, Zhaolin Cai, Ke Gu, Xiongkuo Min, Xiaoping Zhang, Guangtao Zhai
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Omnidirectional videos (ODVs) capture dynamic environments with a full $360^\circ$ field of view, providing rich spatial context and temporal dynamics for applications such as virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), and embodied intelligence. Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made great progress in spatial and temporal understanding, yet their ability to comprehend the immersive 360 spatio-temporal information conveyed by ODVs remains largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we first introduce OmniV-Bench, a Benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate the capabilities of MLLMs on spatio-temporal Omnidirectional Video understanding. OmniV-Bench contains 4,123 high-quality ODVs collected from diverse scenes, along with 8,923 human-annotated question-answer pairs spanning 11 fine-grained tasks across three categories, i.e., general, spatial, and spatio-temporal understanding. Extensive benchmark experiments reveal that the spatio-temporal complexity of ODVs poses significant challenges for current MLLMs. To address these limitations, we further propose OmniV-Harness, an agentic harness that enhances ODV understanding through viewpoint-guided evidence initialization and task-relevant evidence acquisition with adaptive tool use. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that OmniV-Harness consistently improves immersive spatio-temporal reasoning ability of MLLMs. Both OmniV-Bench and OmniV-Harness will be released upon publication.



### WildAnySplat: Feed-Forward 3D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31023v1
- **DOI**: 10.1145/3829340.3842323
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31023v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 17:05:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 17:05:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Richard Shaw, Arthur Moreau, Athanasios Papaioannou, Thomas Tanay, Zhensong Zhang, Eduardo Pérez-Pellitero
- **Comment**: Accepted to SIGGRAPH Asia 2026 (Conference Papers). Project page: https://wildanysplat.github.io/ ; Supplementary material: https://wildanysplat.github.io/wildanysplat_supp.pdf
- **Journal**: Proceedings of the SIGGRAPH Asia 2026 Conference Papers (SA Conference Papers '26), ACM, 2026, 11 pages
- **Summary**: We present WildAnySplat, a feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that enables robust novel-view synthesis from in-the-wild multi-view images. Unlike controlled captures, real-world images often contain multi-view inconsistencies caused by transient occluders, illumination changes, and camera ISP, which degrade the quality of existing feed-forward reconstruction methods.   To address this challenge, we introduce a student-teacher framework that learns transient-robust and appearance-consistent 3D representations. A teacher network operates on clean images, while a student processes the corresponding in-the-wild inputs. The teacher provides clean supervision to guide the student toward a consistent canonical representation. Since paired clean and in-the-wild data are scarce, we use a hybrid data strategy, combining real paired captures, synthetic augmentations, and pseudo-clean labels generated from optimization-based reconstructions, bridging the domain gap between synthetic and real-world data.   We adopt a transformer architecture with dedicated DPT heads for camera pose, depth, and Gaussian prediction. We additionally predict per-view transient masks to reweight photometric supervision and suppress unreliable Gaussians. To account for cross-view appearance variation, we incorporate a bilateral grid module that models per-image color transformations and enables recovery of a canonical radiance representation.   Experiments on challenging in-the-wild benchmarks show that WildAnySplat improves robustness to transient occlusions and appearance variation while retaining the fast inference of feed-forward Gaussian Splatting.



### Token Pruning Needs a Protected Sink Token: Coherent Spatial Aggregation in Vision Transformers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31025v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31025v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 17:06:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 17:06:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Julian Wyatt, James Willoughby, Allison Clement, Irina Voiculescu
- **Comment**: Code: https://github.com/Julian-Wyatt/MCTP
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) are a predominant architecture for computer vision, with token reduction emerging as a popular paradigm to improve their efficiency. Prior work compares reduction algorithms, but not how models respond to them, nor how their response changes with model depth. We propose Monte Carlo Token Pruning (MCTP), which estimates each token's impact on the prediction when tokens are explicitly removed during the forward pass. Applying MCTP to recent pretrained models, we observe that most, such as DINOv3, maintain a similar ranking of token importance at every depth (high Coherent Aggregation), while SigLIP 2 does not. This coherence requires predictions to survive pruning, which depends on keeping high-norm "sink" tokens: prefix tokens are kept by design, while prefix-free SigLIP 2 forms them on patch tokens, which pruning may remove. We show that in removing DINOv3's prefix tokens, its predictions and coherence collapse, while protecting only 5 tokens for SigLIP 2 cuts the token impact outside the object's bounding box by up to 62%. Beyond this, MCTP offers a post-hoc method to evaluate how a pretrained ViT responds to pruning, and suggests that token reduction methods should keep "sinks".



### Déjà VAE: When Do Autoencoder Traces Reveal Generated Images?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31036v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31036v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 17:09:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 17:09:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Saugat Pandey
- **Comment**: 29 pages, 10 figures, 7 tables. Code and per-image scores: https://github.com/iamsaugatpandey/deja-vae
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: A latent diffusion model's output has already passed through its autoencoder, so a second round trip changes it very little. This "déjà vu" underlies reconstruction-based detectors of generated images; we analyse when it reveals a generated image and when it does not. Read by a frozen vision foundation model without training, the trace is strongest in intermediate layers, and, unlike pixel-level reconstruction error, it treats blur as a sign of a real image. It belongs to the generator's exact decoder: on Synthbuster, the exact decoders of Stable Diffusion 1.3, 1.4, 2 and XL are the strongest of eight autoencoders and catch 94-100% of their generators' images at a 5% false-alarm rate (SDXL's version identified post hoc), while near-identical versions catch 7-42%. A detector trained only on real photographs and their autoencoder copies matches one trained on 162k generated images on GenImage (+2.3 points, 95% CI [0.9, 3.7]) and complements it in fusion, but one resize with JPEG re-save, or a mild blur, costs it 19-27 points. Two lessons concern benchmarks. The in-the-wild benchmark Chameleon can be solved from image width alone. And on Synthbuster, how the real photographs are matched to the generated images' size decides whether our detector leads or trails (+4.4 or -9.7 points): the matching protocol alone can reverse a detector comparison. Code and per-image scores: https://github.com/iamsaugatpandey/deja-vae



### Distill the Correction, Not the Rewrite: Hindsight Twins for Self-Improving Text-to-Image Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31048v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31048v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 17:14:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 17:14:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiaze Li, Jingyang Chen, Wenhao Zhao, Haoran Xu, Zhenlong Yuan, Dianmo Sheng, Yuanxing Zhang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Text-to-image models can learn from their own mistakes by distilling a self-teacher conditioned on a vision-language critic's revised prompt. However, such self-teachers barely improve alignment, and both they and their hindsight-contrast variants absorb the critic's unintended additions to the scene, such as lighting or scenery. We attribute this to two factors: (1) the prompt already specifies the desired fix, so a useful revision must go beyond the failed span, often rewriting the entire scene and adding details that overshadow the fix; (2) although the critic localizes the error to a single span, self-teachers condition on the rewrite, entangling the intended fix with unintended additions. Motivated by these insights, we propose Hindsight Twins (HinT), which fills the critic's revision template once with the intended fix and once with the observed error and distills only their velocity difference, anchored at the user's prompt. Because text encoders spread the edit of a single slot to all other tokens, HinT further aligns the twins' token states outside the slot. Across Qwen-Image-2512, SD3.5-M, and FLUX.2-klein, HinT keeps the rate of critic-induced bias at the base-model level while matching or exceeding the alignment gains of prior self-distillation, and it keeps improving over twelve rounds of self-training where contrastive baselines decay.



### RoFE: Rotated Frozen Embeddings for Low-Data Multimodal Tabular Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31049v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV, stat.ML
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31049v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-23 17:14:55+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-23 17:14:55+00:00
- **Authors**: Yihao Qiao, Jiajian Ma, Wushuang Rui, Abhishek Jha, Aarti Darji, Yiqiu Shen
- **Comment**: Code: https://github.com/yihao-qiao/RoFE. Project page: https://yihao-qiao.github.io/RoFE
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal tabular learning combines tabular data with images and text, often in settings where labeled data are scarce. Tabular foundation models such as prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) offer a promising solution via in-context learning without task-specific training. However, modern image and text encoders produce embeddings with hundreds or thousands of dimensions, far exceeding the number of features encountered during PFN pretraining. Existing approaches bridge this gap using principal component analysis (PCA) to compress frozen embeddings. Surprisingly, we find that this PCA interface can underperform simple linear classifiers when labeled examples are scarce, even though PFNs are designed for small datasets. We identify the underlying failure mechanism: the PFN's per-column standardization removes the variances of the PCA scores. When the PCA keeps every direction of the labeled examples, the examples collapse into a regular simplex in which all pairwise distances are equal. To address this problem, we propose Rotated Frozen Embeddings (RoFE), a training-free method that rotates PCA scores before PFN inference. We theoretically show that rotation makes the column variances nearly equal, so that standardization approximately preserves the embedding geometry.
